4.1.2.
Splitting morphisms and comodule structures

The stated skein functor has a good behavior for all three operations that we defined on
\mathcal{M}
. First, it is an immediate consequence of the definition that
\mathcal{S}_{q}:(\mathcal{M},\bigsqcup)\to(\operatorname{Mod}_{k},\otimes_{k})
is monoidal. Next for
a,b
two distinct boundary discs of
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}
, there is a linear map

\theta_{a\#b}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{a\#b})\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}),

named
splitting morphism
defined in
[
Le18
,
BL
]
defined as follows. Let
c\subset M_{a\#b}
be the common image of
a
and
b
. For
[T_{0},s_{0}]\in\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{a\#b})
, isotope
T_{0}
such that it intersects
c
transversally and such that the framing of every point of
T_{0}\cap c
points in the height direction.
Let
T\subset M
be the framed tangle obtained by cutting
T_{0}
along
c
.
Any two states
s_{a}:\partial_{a}T\rightarrow\{-,+\}
and
s_{b}:\partial_{b}T\rightarrow\{-,+\}
give rise to a state
(s_{a},s_{0},s_{b})
on
T
.
Both the sets
\partial_{a}T
and
\partial_{b}T
are in canonical bijection with the set
T_{0}\cap c
by the map quotient map
M\to M_{a\#b}
. Hence the two sets of states
s_{a}
and
s_{b}
are both in canonical bijection with the set
\mathrm{St}(c):=\{s:c\cap T_{0}\rightarrow\{-,+\}\}
.

Definition 4.3
.

(
[
Le18
,
BL
]
)
The
splitting morphism

\theta_{a\#b}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{a\#b})\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
is the linear map given, for any
(T_{0},s_{0})
as above, by:

\theta_{a\#b}\left([T_{0},s_{0}]\right):=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{St}(c)}[T,(s,s_{0},s)].

Figure 11.
An illustration of the splitting morphism
\theta_{a\#b}
.

Theorem 4.4
.

(
[
Le18
, Theorem
3.1
]
)
When
\mathbf{M}
is a thickened surface, then
\theta_{a\#b}
is an injective morphism of algebras.

Recall that the bigon
\mathbb{B}
is a thickened disc with two boundary arcs, say
a_{L}
and
a_{R}
.
For
\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\in\{-,+\}
, let
\alpha_{\varepsilon\varepsilon^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
be the class of the stated arc with arc
\alpha
connecting
a_{L}
to
a_{R}
with state
\varepsilon
on
\alpha\cap a_{L}
and state
\varepsilon^{\prime}
on
\alpha\cap a_{R}
, i.e.
\alpha_{\varepsilon\varepsilon^{\prime}}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{BigonArc.eps}}}}}}}$}
.
By gluing two copies
\mathbb{B}\bigsqcup\mathbb{B}^{\prime}
of the bigon together, by identifying
a_{R}
with
a_{L}^{\prime}
, we get another bigon. The splitting morphism

\Delta:=\theta_{a_{R}\#a_{L}^{\prime}}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})^{\otimes 2}\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B}),

endows
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
with a Hopf algebra structure with coproduct
\Delta
and counit
\epsilon\begin{pmatrix}\alpha{++}&\alpha_{+-}\\
\alpha_{-+}&\alpha_{--}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
has a structure of cobraided Hopf algebra where the co-R matrix
\mathbf{r}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})^{\otimes 2}\to k
is defined by the formula

\mathbf{r}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleHTwistL.eps}}}}}}}$}\otimes\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TanglePrime.eps}}}}}}}$}\right):=\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleCobraiding.eps}}}}}}}$}\right),

The coribbon structure is given by the (positive) co-twist

\Theta\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleHTwistL.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTwistPos.eps}}}}}}}$}\right).

The half-coribbon structure on
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
is defined by the half-twist
t:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\to k
:

t\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleHTwistL.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\varepsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleHTwist.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\varepsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleHTwistR.eps}}}}}}}$}\right).

The rotation operator
\operatorname{rot}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
can be visualized as a
90
degrees rotation (hence the name) as follows:

\operatorname{rot}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleHTwistL.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleRotation.eps}}}}}}}$}\right).

Therefore, the antipode can be depicted graphically as:

S\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleHTwistL.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleAntipode.eps}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleAntipode2.eps}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleAntipode3.eps}}}}}}}$}.

Theorem 4.5
.

(
[
Le18
]
,
[
KQ19
]
,
[
CL19
]
,
[
Hai22
]
)
There exists an isomorphism of half-coribbon Hopf algebras
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\cong\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
sending the generators
\alpha_{++},\alpha_{+-},\alpha_{-+},\alpha_{--}
to
a,b,c,d
respectively, where
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
is equipped with the half-coribbon structure given by
t_{1}
.

Let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}
and
b
a boundary disc of
\mathbf{M}
. By gluing the bigon
\mathbb{B}
to
\mathbf{M}
while identifying
a_{R}
with
b
we get a marked
3
-manifold isomorphic to
\mathbf{M}
so, identifying
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
with
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
using the isomorphism of Theorem
4.5
, the splitting morphism

\Delta_{c}^{L}:=\theta_{a_{R}\#c}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})\to\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\otimes\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{M})

endows
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
with a structure of left
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
-comodule. Similarly, while gluing
b
with
a_{L}
we get left comodule map:

\Delta_{c}^{R}:=\theta_{c\#a_{L}}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})\to\mathcal{S}_{A}(\mathbf{M})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}].

The comodule map
\Delta_{c}^{L}
is depicted in Figure
12
. Note that the two comodules are related by the functor
\operatorname{rot}_{*}:\operatorname{LComod}_{\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]}\to\operatorname{RComod}_{\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]}
, i.e.
\Delta_{c}^{R}=\tau\circ(\operatorname{rot}\otimes id)\circ\Delta_{c}^{L}
.

Therefore, the stated skein functor restricts to functors:

\mathcal{S}_{q}:\mathcal{M}^{(n)}\to(\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}])^{\otimes n}-\operatorname{RComod}.

Figure 12.
An illustration of the comodule maps
\Delta_{c}^{L},\Delta_{c}^{R}
.

4.1.3.
The quantum fusion operation

Definition 4.6
.

Let
(A,\mu_{A},\eta,\Delta,\epsilon_{A},S,\mathbf{r})
be a cobraided Hopf algebra and
C\in\mathrm{RComod}_{A\otimes A}
with comodule map
\Delta_{A\otimes A}:C\to C\otimes A\otimes A
.
Write
\Delta^{1}:=(id\otimes\epsilon\otimes id)\circ\Delta_{A\otimes A}:C\to C\otimes A
and
\Delta^{2}:(\epsilon\otimes id\otimes id)\circ\Delta_{A\otimes A}:C\to C\otimes A
.
The
quantum fusion

{C}_{1\circledast 2}
is the comodule in
\mathrm{RComod}_{A}
where

(1)

C_{1\circledast 2}=C
as a
k
-module;

(2)

The comodule map is
\Delta_{A}:=(id\otimes\mu_{A})\circ\Delta_{A\otimes A}
.

Moreover if
(C,\mu,\epsilon)
is an algebra object in
\mathrm{RComod}_{A\otimes A}
, its quantum fusion has a structure of algebra object
(C_{1\circledast 2},\mu_{1\circledast 2},\epsilon_{1\circledast 2})
in
\mathrm{RComod}_{A}
where
\epsilon_{1\circledast 2}=\epsilon
and the product is the composition

\mu_{1\circledast 2}:C\otimes C\xrightarrow{\Delta_{1}\otimes\Delta_{2}}C\otimes A\otimes C\otimes A\xrightarrow{id\otimes\tau_{A,C}\otimes id}C\otimes C\otimes A\otimes A\xrightarrow{\mu\otimes\mathbf{r}}C.

Remark 4.7
.

(1)

If
V
and
W
are two algebra objects in
\mathrm{RComod}_{A}
, then
V\otimes_{k}W
is an algebra object in
\mathrm{RComod}_{A\otimes A}
and its quantum fusion
(V\otimes_{k}W)_{1\circledast 2}
is the braided tensor product
V\overline{\otimes}W
of Definition
3.7
.

(2)

When
A
is a deformation quantization of some Poisson-Lie group
G
and
C
is the deformation quantization of some (smooth) Poisson
G
-variety, it is proved in
[
Kor22
, Theorem
13
]
that, at the semi-classical level, the quantum fusion operation recovers Alekseev-Malkin’s (classical) fusion operation as defined in
[
AM94
]
(see
[
Kor21
, Section
4.3
]
for details). This explains the name ”quantum fusion operation”.

Now consider a marked surface
\mathbf{M}_{a\circledast b}
obtained by fusioning two boundary disc
a
and
b
of
\mathbf{M}
. Recall that
\mathbb{T}
is a ball with three boundary discs, say
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
and that
\mathbf{M}_{a\circledast b}
is obtained from
\mathbf{M}\bigsqcup\mathbb{T}
by gluing
a
to
e_{1}
and
b
to
e_{2}
.
Define a linear map
\Psi_{a\circledast b}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{a\#b})
by
\Psi_{a\circledast b}([T,s]^{\mathfrak{o}}):=[T^{\prime},s^{\prime}]
where
(T^{\prime},s^{\prime})
is obtained from
(T,s)
by gluing to each point of
T\cap a
a straight line in
\mathbb{T}
between
e_{1}
and
e_{3}
and by gluing to each point of
T\cap b
a straight line in
\mathbb{T}
between
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
as illustrated in Figure
13
.

Figure 13.
An illustration of
\Psi_{a\circledast b}
.

Theorem 4.8
.

(
[
CL19
,
Hig
,
LS
,
Kor22
]
) The linear map
\Psi_{a\circledast b}
is an isomorphism of
k
-modules which identifies
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{a\circledast b})
with the quantum fusion
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})_{a\circledast b}
. If
\mathbf{M}
is a thickened marked surface, then
\Psi_{a\circledast b}
is an isomorphism of algebras.

Theorem
4.8
was proved by Costantino-Lê in
[
CL19
, Theorem
4.13
]
in the particular case where
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}\times I\bigsqcup\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}\times I
is the disjoint union of two thickened marked surfaces with
a
in
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{1}
and
b
in
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{2}
. Another proof was proposed by Higgins in
[
Hig
]
(for the
\operatorname{SL}_{3}
stated skein algebras). As proved independently in
[
LS
, Proposition
7.6
]
and
[
Kor22
, Theorem
2.7
]
, Higgins’ proof extends word-by-word to the more general context of Theorem
4.8
.

Corollary 4.9
.

The functors
\mathcal{S}_{q}:(\mathcal{M}^{(1)},\wedge)\to(\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}},\overline{\otimes})
and
\mathcal{S}_{q}:(\operatorname{MS}^{(1)},\wedge)\to(\operatorname{Alg}(\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}),\overline{\otimes})
are lax monoidal.

4.2.
Costantino-Lê’s skein interpretation of the transmutation

Since
\mathcal{S}_{q}:\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}\to\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
is monoidal, the image by
\mathcal{S}_{q}
of the Hopf algebra object
\mathcal{H}
is a Hopf algebra object
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
. Clearly, its algebra structure is the same as the one in Definition
4.1
. We still denote by
\operatorname{ad}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{2})
the image of
\operatorname{ad}
by
\mathcal{S}_{q}
turning
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
into a comodule over itself in the braided sense. Define a linear isomorphism
f:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
, with inverse
f^{-1}
, by the formula:

f\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleHTwistL.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogon.eps}}}}}}}$},\quad f^{-1}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogon2.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleHTwistL2.eps}}}}}}}$}.

Clearly,
f
sends skein relations in
\mathbb{B}
to skein relations in
\mathbf{H}_{1}
so
f
is well defined. The following is a slight reformulation of Costantino-Lê’s skein interpretation of the transmutation in
[
CL19
]
. Since our conventions are different and since the details of the proof will be crucial in the rest of the paper (and are left to the reader in
[
CL19
]
) we reformulate slightly differently and reprove their result.

Theorem 4.10
.

The linear isomorphism
f
is an isomorphism
f:B\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
of Hopf algebra objects in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
between the transmutation of
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
and
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
. Moreover,
f
intertwines the braided adjoint coaction
\operatorname{Ad}^{B}
and
\operatorname{ad}
.

Corollary 4.11
.

(
[
CL19
,
Fai20
]
)
One has an isomorphism
\widetilde{f}:B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
of Hopf algebra objects in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
characterized by:

\widetilde{f}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-A^{5/2}\\
A^{1/2}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{+}^{+}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{+}^{-}\\
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{-}^{+}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{-}^{-}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-A^{5/2}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{-}^{+}&-A^{5/2}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{-}^{-}\\
A^{1/2}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{+}^{+}&A^{1/2}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{+}^{-}\end{pmatrix}.

So one gets an algebra isomorphism
(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\overline{\otimes}n}\cong\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{n})
and the two functors
\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=1.67639pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=1.46388pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}:\operatorname{BT}\to\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
and
Q_{B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}}:\operatorname{BT}\to\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
are isomorphic.

Remark 4.12
.

(1)

(Comparison with Costantino-Lê)
Let
\Theta:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})\to\mathcal{S}_{q^{-1}}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
be the (involutive) isomorphism sending a class
[T,s]
for which
s:\partial T\to\{v_{-},v_{+}\}
is valued in the standard basis, to
[\varphi(T),s\circ\varphi^{-1}]
, where
\varphi:\mathbb{D}_{1}\times[-1,1]\cong\mathbb{D}_{1}\times[-1,1]
sends
(x,t)
to
(x,1-t)
. Then
\Theta
is an anti-morphism of algebras, i.e.
\Theta(xy)=\Theta(y)\Theta(x)
(see
[
Le18
]
where
\Theta
is called
reflexion involution
for details). Recall from Definition
3.3
the (involutive) anti-morphism of algebras
C_{t}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
. The composition
\Theta\circ f\circ C_{t}
is an algebra morphism between
B\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
and
\mathcal{S}_{q^{-1}}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
. Changing
q
to
q^{-1}
, we get an isomorphism
g:B_{q^{-1}}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
characterized by the formula

g\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimpleUp.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{+}^{+}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimpleUp.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{+}^{-}\\
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimpleUp.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{-}^{+}&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimpleUp.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{-}^{-}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-A^{5/2}\\
A^{1/2}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}A^{1/2}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimpleUp.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{+}^{-}&-A^{5/2}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimpleUp.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{+}^{+}\\
A^{1/2}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimpleUp.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{-}^{-}&-A^{5/2}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimpleUp.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{-}^{+}\end{pmatrix}.

Costantino and Lê proved in
[
CL19
, Proposition
4.17
]
that
g
is a surjective morphism of Hopf algebras (there is a typo in
[
CL19
, Proposition
4.17
]
where
-A^{5/2}
were incidentally replaced by
-A^{3/2}
). The authors also claim the injectivity of
g
without giving any argument.

(2)

(Comparison with Faitg) In
[
Fai20
]
, Faitg considered an algebra denoted by
\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
named
quantum moduli algebra
which appeared originally in
[
AGS95
,
AGS96
,
BR95
,
BR96
]
and proved in
[
Fai20
, Lemma
5.6
]
the existence of an isomorphism of algebras
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})^{op}\cong\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
, where
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})^{op}
is the skein algebra with opposite product (tangle are stacked from bottom to top in
[
Fai20
]
instead of top to bottom). By comparing the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
in
[
Fai20
, Equation
51
]
with Equation (
3
) in Section
3.3
, one sees that
\mathcal{L}_{0,1}
is canonically isomorphic to
B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
and that Faitg isomorphism corresponds to the isomorphism
h:=\widetilde{f}\circ C_{t}:B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\cong\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})^{op}
. To prove that
h
is isomorphism, Faitg constructed an explicit inverse (see also
[
Kor20
]
where an alternative proof is presented using PBW bases).

(3)

The two above items imply that Theorem
4.10
is simply a reformulation of the theorems of Costantino-Lê and Faitg. However the previous proofs are made by blind computations (left to the reader in
[
CL19
]
), using the knowledge of explicit vectorial bases for stated skein algebras. The purpose of this subsection is to provide a more conceptual proof by directly reinterpreting Majid’s general formulas (
1
) and (
2
) of Definition
3.7
in the skein framework. This idea is obviously present in Costantino and Lê’s work and no originality is claimed in this section. The advantage of the above proof is that it generalizes to arbitrary group
G
. Indeed, the stated skein functor can be generalized to every reducible algebraic group
G
(the case detailed in this paper is
G=\operatorname{SL}_{2}
) as
\mathcal{S}_{q}^{G}:\mathcal{M}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{k}
where
k=k_{G}
. The (easy) case
G=\mathbb{C}^{*}
is defined in
[
KQ22
]
, the
\operatorname{SL}_{3}
case was studied by Higgins in
[
Hig
]
, the
\operatorname{SL}_{n}
case was studied by Lê-Sikora
[
LS
]
and the general case will appear in the next forthcoming paper
[
CKL
]
. Unlike the
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
case, finding explicit bases for stated skein algebras in general is quite difficult (see
[
Hig
]
in the
\operatorname{SL}_{3}
case), hence the necessity of finding bases-independent proofs in order to work for a general group. The proofs of Theorems
4.5
(
\mathcal{S}_{q}^{G}(\mathbb{B})\cong\mathcal{O}_{q}G
) and
4.8
extend to any group, so does the above proof of Theorem
4.10
. Even though we only discuss the
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
case, the whole paper is designed so that all results and proofs extend straightforwardly to every (connected reducible complex) group
G
.

Notations 4.13
.

In order to simplify the computations, a generic stated tangle

in the bigon will simply be denoted by
v\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}w
, where it is understood
T
has arbitrary numbers
n,m\geq 0
of left and right endpoints and that
v\in V^{n}
and
w\in V^{m}
.
Its image

by
f
will be denoted by
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{\operatorname{ht}_{*}(v)}^{w}
, where for
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in V^{n}
we wrote
\operatorname{ht}_{*}(v)=(\operatorname{ht}_{*}(v_{1}),\ldots,\operatorname{ht}_{*}(v_{n}))
.

Proof of Theorem
4.10
.

Let
\underline{\mu},\underline{S}
be the transmuted products and antipode of
B\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
as in Definition
3.7
.

f

is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
-comodule:

Let us prove that
(f\otimes id)\circ\operatorname{Ad}\circ f^{-1}
coincides with the (right) comodule structure of
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
. Let
x=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT.eps}}}}}}}$}{}^{b}_{a}
so that
f^{-1}(x)=\operatorname{ht}_{*}^{-1}(a)\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}b
. By definition,
\operatorname{Ad}=(id\otimes\mu)(\tau\otimes id)(S\otimes id\otimes id)\Delta^{(2)}
. Now

\Delta^{(2)}(f^{-1}(x))=\sum_{ij}\operatorname{ht}_{*}^{-1}(a)\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}i\otimes i\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}j\otimes j\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}b.

So

(S\otimes id\otimes id)\Delta^{(2)}(f^{-1}(x))=\sum_{ij}\operatorname{ht}^{-1}(i)\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}a\otimes i\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}j\otimes j\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}b,

from which we get

\operatorname{Ad}(f^{-1}(x))=\sum_{ij}i\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}j\otimes{}_{\operatorname{ht}^{-1}(i)}^{j}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleDouble.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{a}^{b},

so

(f\otimes id)\circ\operatorname{Ad}\circ f^{-1}(x)=\sum_{ij}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{\operatorname{ht}_{*}(i)}^{j}\otimes{}_{\operatorname{ht}^{-1}(i)}^{j}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleDouble.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{a}^{b}=\sum_{ij}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{i}^{j}\otimes{}_{i}^{j}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleDouble.eps}}}}}}}$}{}_{a}^{b}.

Thus we recover the comodule structure of
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
.

f

is an isomorphism of algebras:

Let us prove that
f\circ\underline{\mu}\circ(f^{-1}\otimes f^{-1})
coincides with the product of
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
. Let
x=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT.eps}}}}}}}$}{}^{b}_{a}
and
y=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT2.eps}}}}}}}$}{}^{d}_{c}
so that
f^{-1}(x)\otimes f^{-1}(y)=\operatorname{ht}_{*}^{-1}(a)\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}b\otimes\operatorname{ht}_{*}^{-1}(c)\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleSimple2.eps}}}}}}}$}d
. Equation (
1
) in Definition
3.7
reads:

\underline{\mu}\left(f^{-1}(x)\otimes f^{-1}(y)\right)=\sum_{i,j,k}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedProd1.eps}}}}}}}$}\quad\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedProd2.eps}}}}}}}$}\right).

Therefore:

f\circ\underline{\mu}\circ(f^{-1}\otimes f^{-1})(x\otimes y)=\sum_{i,j,k}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedProd3.eps}}}}}}}$}\quad\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedProd2.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedProd4.eps}}}}}}}$},

so we recover the product in the skein algebra
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
.

f

is an isomorphism of co-algebras:

Let us prove that
(f\wedge f)\circ\Delta\circ f^{-1}
coincides with the image by
\mathcal{S}_{q}
of the coproduct in Figure
5
. Let
x=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT.eps}}}}}}}$}{}^{b}_{a}
so that
f^{-1}(x)=\operatorname{ht}_{*}^{-1}(a)\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}b
. Then

\Delta(f^{-1}(x))=\sum_{i}\operatorname{ht}_{*}^{-1}(a)\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}i\otimes i\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleVSimple.eps}}}}}}}$}b,

so

(f\wedge f)(\Delta(f^{-1}(x)))=\sum_{i}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedCoprod1.eps}}}}}}}$}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedCoprod2.eps}}}}}}}$}.

Skein interpretation of the antipode:

At this stage we have proved that
f
is an isomorphism of bialgebras so it preserves the antipode. However, it is instructive to verify directly that indeed the rather abstract Equation (
2
) in Definition
3.7
has a very natural topological interpretation given in Figure
5
. Let
x=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT.eps}}}}}}}$}{}^{b}_{a}
as before.
Then Equation (
2
) reads:

f\circ\overline{S}(f^{-1}(x))=\sum_{ijk}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT.eps}}}}}}}$}{}^{\operatorname{ht}_{*}(i)}_{j}\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedAntipod1.eps}}}}}}}$}\right).

Now, for a basis vector
v_{k}
, then

\operatorname{ht}_{*}\circ\operatorname{ht}(v_{k})=\sum_{lm}\left<v_{l}^{*},\operatorname{ht}_{*}(v_{m})\right>\left<v_{m}^{*},\operatorname{ht}(v_{k})\right>v_{m}=\sum_{lm}\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedAntipod4.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedAntipod5.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)v_{m}.

Therefore:

f\circ\overline{S}(f^{-1}(x))=\sum_{ijklm}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT.eps}}}}}}}$}{}^{i}_{j}\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedAntipod4.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedAntipod5.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedAntipod2.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedAntipod3.eps}}}}}}}$}.

f

preserves the braiding:

Let
\Psi_{0}:B\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})^{\otimes 2}\to B\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})^{\otimes 2}
be the braiding from item
(5)
of Definition
3.3
associated to
B\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
seen as a comodule over
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
via
\operatorname{Ad}
. Said differently,

\Psi_{0}=(\tau\otimes r)(id\otimes\tau\otimes id)(\operatorname{Ad}\otimes\operatorname{Ad}).

Let us prove that
(f\wedge f)\circ\Psi_{0}\circ(f^{-1}\otimes f^{-1})
coincides with the image by
\mathcal{S}_{q}
of the braiding
\Psi
of Figure
5
. Let
x=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT.eps}}}}}}}$}{}^{b}_{a}
and
y=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleMonogonSimpleT2.eps}}}}}}}$}{}^{d}_{c}
. Using the above expression for
\Psi_{0}
, we compute:

(f\wedge f)\circ\Psi_{0}\circ(f^{-1}\otimes f^{-1})(x\otimes y)=\sum_{ijkl}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedBraiding1.eps}}}}}}}$}\epsilon\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedBraiding2.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleTransmutedBraiding3.eps}}}}}}}$}.

Now, the fact that
f
intertwines the comodule maps
\operatorname{Ad}^{B}
and
\operatorname{ad}
comes from that it intertwines the product, coproduct, antipode and braiding and from the fact that both comodules map are defined with the same formula, namely that

\operatorname{Ad}^{B}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{AdB.eps}}}}}}}$}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\operatorname{ad}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{Aad.eps}}}}}}}$}.

∎

4.3.
Relating
\mathcal{S}_{q}
to
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=1.67639pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=1.46388pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}:\operatorname{BT}\to\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
be the restriction of the functor
\mathcal{S}_{q}:\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}\to\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
and
i:\operatorname{BT}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}
the inclusion. Then
\mathcal{S}_{q}=Lan_{i}\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=1.67639pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=1.46388pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}
, i.e.
\mathcal{S}_{q}
is the left Kan extension lying in the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{BT}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=1.65987pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=1.65987pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.67867pt,depth=1.20322pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=1.91333pt,depth=1.0247pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}}

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}
and consider the left Kan extension of Section
3.1
:

L(\mathbf{M})=Lan_{i}\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.43195pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=1.67639pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{q}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=1.46388pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}(\mathbf{M}):=\int^{n\geq 0}k[\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{H}_{n},\mathbf{M})]\otimes\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{n}).

Define a linear morphism
F^{(n)}:k[\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathbf{H}_{n},\mathbf{M})]\otimes\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{n})\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
by
F^{(n)}(f\otimes x):=f_{*}(x)
. For
g:\mathbf{H}_{n}\to\mathbf{H}_{m}
a morphism in
\operatorname{BT}
,
f:\mathbf{H}_{m}\to\mathbf{M}
an embedding and
x\in\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{n})
then
F^{(n)}(f\circ g\otimes x)=f_{*}\circ g_{*}(x)=F^{(m)}(f\otimes g_{*}x)
so the morphisms
F^{(n)}
induce a linear map
F_{\mathbf{M}}:L(\mathbf{M})\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
. Clearly the morphisms
F_{\mathbf{M}}
are natural in
\mathbf{M}
so define a natural morphism
F:L\to\mathcal{S}_{q}
. Let us define an inverse morphism
G
. Let
(T,s)
be a stated tangle in
\mathbf{M}
. Let
\mathcal{E}\subset\bigsqcup_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{M}^{(1)}}(\mathbf{H}_{n},\mathbf{M})
be the subset of embeddings
f:\mathbf{H}_{n}\to\mathbf{M}
such that
T
is included in the image of
f
. For
f,g\in\mathcal{E}
, write
f\prec g
if the image of
f
is included in the image of
g
. First, since
M
is connected, the set
\mathcal{E}
is not empty. Next, the partially ordered set
(\mathcal{E},\prec)
is filtrant: for
f,g\in\mathcal{E}
, one can always find
h\in\mathcal{E}
such that the image of
h
contains both the images of
f
and
g
.
For
f:\mathbf{H}_{n}\to\mathbf{M}
an embedding in
\mathcal{E}
, consider the stated tangle
(T_{f},s_{f})
in
\mathbf{H}_{n}
such that
(T,s)=(f(T_{f}),s_{f}\circ f^{-1})
and define
G_{f}([T,s]):=[f\otimes[T_{f},s_{f}]]\in L(\mathbf{M})
. For
f,g
two elements in
\mathcal{E}
with
f:\mathbf{H}_{n}\to\mathbf{M}
and
g:\mathbf{H}_{m}\to\mathbf{M}
such that
f\prec g
, then one can find
h:\mathbf{H}_{n}\to\mathbf{H}_{m}
such that
f=g\circ h
. Thus one has
[T_{g},s_{g}]=h_{*}[T_{f},s_{f}]
so
G_{g}([T,s])=[g\otimes h_{*}[T_{f},s_{f}]]=[g\circ h,[T_{f},s_{f}]]=G_{f}([T,s])
. Since
\prec
is filtrant, this proves that
G_{f}([T,s])
is independant of
f
, let us denote it by
G_{\mathbf{M}}(T,s)
which is clearly invariant by isotopy. Extend
G_{\mathbf{M}}
linearly to the free
k
module generated by stated tangles in
\mathbf{M}
. Let us prove that
G_{\mathbf{M}}
passes to the quotient to a linear morphism

G_{\mathbf{M}}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})\to L(\mathbf{M})
, i.e. that it sends the skein relations of Definition
4.1
to
0
. If
X=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}(T^{i},s^{i})
is a linear combination of stated tangles such that
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}[T^{i},s^{i}]=0
in
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
(i.e.
X
is a skein relation), consider an embedding
f:\mathbf{H}_{n}\to\mathbf{M}
such that every
T_{i}
is included in the image of
f
and denote by
(T_{f}^{i},s^{i}_{f})\subset H_{n}
the stated tangle such that
(T^{i},s^{i})=(f(T_{f}^{i}),s_{f}^{i}\circ f^{-1})
as before. By locality of the skein relations,
X_{f}:=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}(T_{f}^{i},s_{f}^{i})
is a skein relation in
\mathbf{H}_{n}
so its class in
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{n})
vanishes. Therefore
G_{\mathbf{M}}(X)=[f\otimes[X_{f}]]=0
so one has a linear map
G_{\mathbf{M}}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})\to L(\mathbf{M})
.

Clearly the maps
G_{\mathbf{M}}
are natural in
\mathbf{M}
so define a natural morphism
G:\mathcal{S}_{q}\to L
. It is a straightforward consequence of the definitions of
F
and
G
that they are inverse to each other.

∎

The following implies Theorem
1.2
.

Corollary 4.15
.

The functors
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}:\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}\to\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{q}:\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}\to\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
are isomorphic.

4.4.
Coinvariant vectors

Let
\mathbf{M}=(M,\iota_{M})\in\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}
and consider the unmarked
3
-manifold
M\in\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\operatorname{c}}
without marked disc. The identity
id_{M}:M\to M
is a marked
3
-manifolds embedding
\iota:M\to\mathbf{M}
so induces a morphism
\iota_{*}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(M)\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
between the usual skein module of
M
to the stated skein module of
\mathbf{M}
. Clearly, the elements of the image of
\iota_{*}
are coinvariant for the
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
coaction. By Theorem
4.14
, the submodule
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})^{coinv}
of coinvariant vectors is isomorphic to Habiro’s quantum character variety. So in order to relate skein module with quantum character variety, we need to understand whether
\iota_{*}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(M)\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})^{coinv}
is an isomorphism or not.
Recall that
k_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}=\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/4}]
and consider the field of fractions
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}:=\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/4})
and the
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
vector space
\mathcal{S}_{q}^{rat}(\mathbf{M}):=\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})\otimes_{k_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
. It is a comodule structure over
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{rat}:=\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}\otimes_{k_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
.

The goal of this subsection is to prove the following:

Theorem 4.16
.

The morphism
\iota_{*}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(M)\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})^{coinv}
is surjective. Moreover, after tensoring by
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
it becomes an isomorphism
\iota_{*}:\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(M)\to\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(\mathbf{M})^{coinv}
.

In particular the kernel of
\iota_{*}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(M)\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})^{coinv}
lies in the torsion submodule of
\mathcal{S}_{q}(M)
.

Corollary 4.17
.

The Kauffman-bracket skein module
\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(M)
is isomorphic to the quantum character variety
\operatorname{Char}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2},rat}(\mathbf{M}):=(\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2},rat}(\mathbf{M}))^{coinv}
while working over the field
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
.

The reason we need to work over the field
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
instead of the ring
k_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
is that the category
\overline{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2},rat}_{q}}
is semi-simple, so its elements are flat.

Definition 4.18
.

(1)

The
quantum plane

\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
is the quotient of the (non commutative)
k
-algebra freely generated by two generators
x
and
y
by the relation
xy=q^{-1}yx
.

(2)

Define a left comodule map
\Delta^{L}:\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\to\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
by the formula:

\Delta^{L}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\otimes\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a\otimes x+b\otimes y\\
c\otimes x+d\otimes y\end{pmatrix}.

(3)

The quantum plane is graded
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]=\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]^{(n)}
where
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]^{(n)}=\operatorname{Span}\left(x^{i}y^{j},i+j=n\right)
and clearly
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]^{(n)}\cdot\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]^{(m)}\subset\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]^{(n+m)}
. The comodule map
\Delta^{L}
restricts to comodule maps
\Delta^{L}_{(n)}:\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]^{(n)}\to\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]^{(n)}
.

Lemma 4.19
.

The set of coinvariant vectors
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]^{\operatorname{coinv}}
is the set
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]^{(0)}\cong k
of scalars.

Proof.

The algebras
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
and
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
are quadratic and quadratic inhomogeneous respectively and they both satisfy the Koszul condition. An easy application of the Diamond lemma for PBW bases implies that they have bases
\mathcal{B}_{1}:=\{x^{i}y^{j},i,j\geq 0\}
and
\mathcal{B}_{2}:=\{a^{n_{a}}b^{n_{b}}d^{n_{d}},n_{a},n_{b},n_{c}\geq 0\}\cup\{a^{n_{a}}c^{n_{c}}d^{n_{d}},n_{a},n_{c},n_{d}\geq 0\}
respectively (see
[
Kas95
]
for details). Therefore
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
has basis
\mathcal{B}:=\mathcal{B}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2}
. For
\mathbf{n}=(n_{x},n_{y},n_{a},n_{b},n_{c},n_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{6}
(here
\mathbb{N}=\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}
), let
z^{\mathbf{n}}:=x^{n_{x}}y^{n_{y}}\otimes a^{n_{a}}b^{n_{b}}c^{n_{c}}d^{n_{d}}\in\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
. Let
\mathcal{N}\subset\mathbb{N}^{6}
be the subset of
6
-tuples such that
n_{b}n_{c}=0
so that
\mathcal{B}=\{z^{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{N}\}
. Equip
\mathcal{N}
with the lexicographic order
\prec
. For
z=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{N}}\alpha_{\mathbf{n}}z^{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\otimes\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
, let
\mathbf{n}_{0}
be the biggest index (for
\prec
) such that
\alpha_{\mathbf{n}_{0}}\neq 0
and define the
leading term
of
z
to be

\operatorname{lt}(z):=\alpha_{\mathbf{n}_{0}}z^{\mathbf{n_{0}}}.

Let
X:=\sum_{i,j\geq 0}x_{i,j}x^{i}y^{j}\in\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
be a coinvariant vector and let
(i_{0},j_{0})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
be the biggest index (for the lexicographic order) such that
x_{i_{0},j_{0}}\neq 0
. By definition,
X
is coinvariant means that

\sum_{i,j\geq 0}x_{i,j}1\otimes x^{i}y^{j}=\sum_{i,j\geq 0}x_{i,j}(a\otimes x+b\otimes y)^{i}(c\otimes x+d\otimes y)^{j}.

Taking the leading terms of each side of this equality, and using the
q
-binomial formula (
[
Kas95
, Proposition
IV.2.2
]
) we obtain the equality:

x_{i_{0},j_{0}}1\otimes x^{i_{0}}y^{j_{0}}=x_{i_{0},j_{0}}a^{i_{0}}c^{j_{0}}\otimes x^{i_{0}}y^{j_{0}}.

Therefore
(i_{0},j_{0})=(0,0)
and
X
is scalar.
∎

Definition 4.20
.

(1)

Recall that
\mathbb{B}
is a ball with two boundary discs
a_{L}
and
a_{R}
. For
T
a tangle in
\mathbb{B}
a
left state
is a map
s^{L}:T\cap a_{L}\to V
.
The
skein quantum plane

\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
is the quotient of the
k
-module freely generated by isotopy classes of left stated tangles
(T,s^{L})
in
\mathbb{B}
by the ideal generated by the skein relations of Definition
4.1
applied either in the interior of
\mathbb{B}
or in a ball intersecting
a_{L}
(so there are no skein relations along
a_{R}
). It has an algebra with product given by stacking tangles like in stated skein algebras.

(2)

Define a left comodule map
\Delta^{L}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{A}^{2})\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\otimes\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{A}^{2})
as follows. For
(T,s^{L})
a left stated tangle choose an arbitrary right stated tangle
s^{R}
so that
[T,(s^{L},s^{R})]
is an element of
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
. Split
T
along a disc as
T=T^{L}\cup T^{R}
such that, by definition of the splitting morphism
\Delta=\theta_{a_{R}\#a^{\prime}_{L}}
, one has
\Delta([T,(s^{L},s^{R})])=\sum_{s}[T_{L},(s^{L},s)]\otimes[T_{R},(s,s^{R})]
. Then define
\Delta^{L}([T,s^{L}]):=\sum_{s}[T_{L},(s^{L},s)]\otimes[T_{R},s]
. This formula clearly does not depend on the choice of
s^{R}
and the fact that
\Delta
does not depend on the choice of the splitting
T=T_{L}\cup T_{R}
implies that
\Delta^{L}
does not depend on this choice either.
The left comodule
\Delta^{L}
is illustrated in Figure
14
.

(3)

Define a filtration
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]=\cup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{F}^{(n)}
, where
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}:=\operatorname{Span}([T,s^{L}],|\partial T\cap a_{L}|\leq n)
. One has
\mathcal{F}^{(n)}\cdot\mathcal{F}^{(m)}\subset\mathcal{F}^{(n+m)}
and
the comodule map
\Delta^{L}
restricts to comodule maps
\Delta^{L}_{(n)}:\mathcal{F}^{(n)}\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\otimes\mathcal{F}^{(n)}
.
Set
\operatorname{Gr}_{0}(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}])=\mathcal{F}_{0}
and for
n\geq 1
, write
\operatorname{Gr}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{F}_{n}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{F}_{n-1}$}\right.}
. The graded algebra
\operatorname{Gr}(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]):=\oplus_{n\geq 0}\operatorname{Gr}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}])
receives, by passing to the quotient, a left
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
comodule structure, so does its graded components
\operatorname{Gr}_{n}(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}])
.

Figure 14.
An illustration of the coproduct in the skein quantum plane.

Recall that
\alpha
is a connected tangle in
\mathbb{B}
(an arc) connecting
a_{L}
to
a_{R}
. Let
\alpha_{+},\alpha_{-}\in\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
be the classes of the right stated tangles
(\alpha,+)
and
(\alpha,-)
.
Recall from Theorem
4.5
the Hopf algebra isomorphism
f_{\mathbb{B}}:\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
sending
a,b,c,d
to
\alpha_{++},\alpha_{+-},\alpha_{-+},\alpha_{--}
respectively.

Let
k[\mathbb{N}]=k[X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n},\ldots]
be the algebra of polynomials with an infinite number of variables and consider

\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[\mathbb{N}]
the free product of
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
with
k[\mathbb{N}]
. Define a comodule map
\Delta^{L}:\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[\mathbb{N}]\to\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\otimes(\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[\mathbb{N}])
whose restriction to
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
is the standard coproduct and such that
\Delta^{L}(X_{i})=1\otimes X_{i}
. Extend the grading to
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[\mathbb{N}]
by stating that
X_{i}
has degree
0
.

Lemma 4.21
.

(1)

There is an isomorphism of graded algebras
f_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}:\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[\mathbb{N}]\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Gr}\left(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\right)
characterized by the fact that
f_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}(x)=\alpha_{+}
,
f_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}(y)=\alpha_{-}
and
f(X_{i})=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{TangleXi.eps}}}}}}}$}
.

(2)

The following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[\mathbb{N}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\otimes(\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[\mathbb{N}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Gr}(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\otimes\operatorname{Gr}\left(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\mathbb{B}}\otimes f_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{L}}

(3)

The submodule
\mathcal{F}^{(0)}
, spanned by left stated tangles
[T,s^{L}]
such that
T\cap a_{L}=\emptyset
, is equal to the submodule of coinvariant vectors of
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
.

Proof.

(1)
The fact that
\alpha_{+}\alpha_{-}=q^{-1}\alpha_{-}\alpha_{+}
follows from the following skein relation:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\pmod{\mathcal{F}_{1}}.

Therefore the graded algebra morphism
f_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}:\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[X]\xrightarrow{\cong}\operatorname{Gr}\left(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\right)
is well defined.
The polynomial algebra
k[\mathbb{N}]
has basis elements
X^{\mathbf{n}}=X_{1}^{n_{1}}\ldots X_{k}^{n_{k}}\ldots
for
\mathbf{n}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
a map with finite support.
Recall the basis
\mathcal{B}_{1}=\{x^{i}y^{j},i,j\geq 0\}
of
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
so that
\widehat{\mathcal{B}_{1}}:=\{b_{1}X^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\ldots b_{k}X^{\mathbf{n}_{k}},k\geq 0,b_{i}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}\}
is a basis of
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[\mathbb{N}]
. We need to show that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}:=f(\widehat{\mathcal{B}_{1}})
is a basis of
\operatorname{Gr}\left(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\right)
in order to prove that
f_{\mathbb{A}^{2}}
is an isomorphism. This is equivalent to proving that
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is a basis of
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
. The proof is a straightforward adaption of Lê’s arguments in the proof of
[
Le18
, Theorem
2.8
]
, based on the Diamond lemma, that we now develop. Identify the ball
\mathbb{B}
with a thickened disc
\mathbb{D}^{2}\times I
and let
\pi:\mathbb{D}^{2}\times I\to\mathbb{D}^{2}
the projection map on
\mathbb{D}^{2}\times\{0\}
. A tangle
T
is in generic position if its framing at every point points in the height direction towards
1
and if the projection
\pi:T\to\pi(T)
only has transverse double points in the interior of
\mathbb{D}^{2}
. A
diagram

D
of
T
is then the data to the planar graph
\pi(T)
together with for each of its double point, the over/under crossing information. A left stated diagram is
(D,s^{L})
with
s^{L}:D\cap a_{L}\to\{v_{-},v_{+}\}
. Note that the states are elements of the basis
\{v_{+},v_{-}\}
instead of arbitrary vectors in
V
.
Fix the orientation
\mathfrak{o}
of both boundary arcs
a_{L},a_{R}
corresponding to the arrows in the pictures
+
-
. Then a stated diagram defines an element
[D,s^{L}]\in\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
and
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]
is the quotient of the free
k
-module generated by (planar) isotopy classes of left stated diagrams by the framed Reidemeister moves together with the skein relations. Let
\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
be the set of left stated diagrams. Then
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is the set of classes of elements
(D,s^{L})
of
\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}
such that
(1)

D
does not have any crossing,
(2)

D
does not have any connected component with both endpoints in
a_{L}
and
(3)
the state
s^{L}
is
\mathfrak{o}
increasing, i.e if
p_{1},p_{2}\in D\cap a_{L}
are such that
p_{1}
is on top
p_{2}
then
s^{L}(p_{1})\geq s^{L}(p_{2})
(here
v_{+}>v_{-}
). Define a binary operation
\to
on
k[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}]
as follows. If
[D,s]\in\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}\in k[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}]
write
D\to E^{\prime}
if they are related by one of the following skein manipulation:

More generally, write
E\to E^{\prime}
if
E=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}[D_{i},s_{i}]
and there exists
i_{0}
with
\alpha_{i_{0}}\neq 0
,
[D_{i_{0}},s_{i_{0}}]\to E_{i_{0}}
related by a skein relation as above and such that
E^{\prime}=\alpha_{i_{0}}E_{i_{0}}+\sum_{i\neq i_{0}}\alpha_{i}E_{i}
. Let
\sim
be the equivalence relation on
k[\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}]
generated by
\to
. Note that two stated diagrams related by framed Reidemeister moves are equivalent for
\sim
(this follows using the relations given by the Kauffman-bracket skein relations), therefore
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathcal{A}]={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$k[\overline{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim$}\right.}
. The arguments in the proof of
[
Le18
, Lemma
2.10
]
extend world-by-world and show that
\to
is terminal and locally confluent, therefore the Diamond lemma implies that the set of initial objects for
\to
is a basis of
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathcal{A}]
. This set is precisely
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
. Since this basis is made of graded elements, its image in
\operatorname{Gr}\left(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathcal{A}]\right)
is a basis as well. Therefore
f_{\mathcal{A}}
is an isomorphism. This concludes the proof of
(1)
.

(2)
It is sufficient to prove the commutativity of the diagram for the generators
x,y,X
of
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[X]
. This follows from the following computations:

(3)
Since
X
is coinvariant by definition, by Lemma
4.19
the subset of coinvariant vectors of
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\star k[\mathbb{N}]
is its graded
0
part, so the same is true for
\operatorname{Gr}(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}])
by
(2)
and since projection
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}]\to\operatorname{Gr}(\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}^{2}])
sends coinvariant vectors to coinvariant vectors and preserves the grading, the results follows.
∎

Definition 4.22
.

(1)

For
n\geq 0
let
[n]
be the
n
-tuple of framed points
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
where
p_{i}:=(0,\frac{i}{n})\in\mathbb{D}^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with framing pointing towards the height direction. For
n,m\geq 0
a
[n]-[m]
tangle is a tangle
T
in
\mathbb{B}
such that
\iota^{-1}_{a_{R}}(\partial T\cap a_{R})=[n]
and
\iota^{-1}_{a_{L}}(\partial T\cap a_{L})=[m]
. The
Temperley-Lieb category

\operatorname{TL}
has objects the non negative integers
n\geq 0
and the set of morphisms
\operatorname{TL}(n,m)
is the quotient of

The composition is obtained by gluing the tangles together.

(2)

Define a right module
F_{M}:\operatorname{TL}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{k}
by letting
F_{M}(n)
be the quotient of the
k
-module freely generated by isotopy classes of tangles
T
in
\mathbf{M}
such that
\iota_{M}^{-1}(T)=[n]
, by the ideal generated by the Kauffman-bracket relations. When
[T^{\prime}]\in\operatorname{TL}(n,m)
is the class of a
[n]-[m]
tangle we define
F_{M}([T]):F_{M}(n)\to F_{M}(m)
to be the linear map sending the class
[T]\in F_{M}(n)
of a tangle in
\mathbf{M}
to
[T^{\prime}\cup T]
obtained by gluing the bigon
\mathbb{B}
to
\mathbf{M}
while gluing
\mathbb{D}_{M}
to
a_{R}
. The functor
F_{M}
was called the
internal skein module
in
[
GJS
]
and its restriction to marked surfaces appeared earlier in
[
BZBJ18
,
Coo19
]
.

(3)

In addition to the filtration of Definition
4.20
, the skein quantum plane
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]
has a graduation
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]=\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{<n>}
, where

\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{<n>}:=\operatorname{Span}\left([T,s^{L}],|\partial T\cap a_{R}|=n\right).

So whereas the filtration of Definition
4.20
is based on the number of intersection points of
T
with
a_{L}
, this graduation counts the number of intersection points of
T
with
a_{R}
.
Clearly
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{<n>}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{<m>}=\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{<n+m>}
, so we have an algebra graduation. The comodule structure of
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]
preserves each graded component so induces comodule maps

\Delta^{L}_{<n>}:\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{<n>}\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\otimes\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{<n>}
.

(4)

Define a left module
F_{\mathbb{A}}:\operatorname{TL}^{op}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{k}
by sending
n
to
\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{<n>}
. The action on morphisms is defined as follows. For
T^{\prime}\in\operatorname{TL}(m,n)
then
F_{\mathbb{A}}(T^{\prime})
sends
[T,s^{L}]\in\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{<n>}
to
[T\cup T^{\prime},s^{L}]
where
T\cup T^{\prime}
is obtained by gluing the bigon
\mathbb{B}
where lives
T
to the bigon
\mathbb{B}^{\prime}
where lives
T^{\prime}
by gluing
a_{R}
to
a^{\prime}_{L}
. The comodules maps
\Delta^{L}_{<n>}
define a natural morphism
\Delta^{L}:F_{\mathbb{A}}\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\otimes F_{\mathbb{A}}
.

Lemma 4.23
.

The functor
\bullet\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{M}:\widehat{\operatorname{TL}}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{k}
, sending a functor
G:\operatorname{TL}^{op}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{k}
to the
k
-module
G\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{M}
, is right exact while working over the ring
k_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
and becomes exact when working over the field
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
.

Proof.

Let
\iota:\operatorname{TL}\to\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
be the ribbon functor sending
[n]
to
V^{\otimes n}
(see
[
Tin
]
for details), which identifies
\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
with the Cauchy closure of
\operatorname{TL}
. Then the functor
\iota^{*}:\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}\to\widehat{\operatorname{TL}}
, defined by
\iota^{*}(G):=G\circ\iota
, is an equivalence of categories. Still denote by
F_{M}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
a fixed lift of
F_{M}
by
\iota^{*}
(unique up to unique isomorphism). It is sufficient to prove that the functor
\bullet\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{M}:\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{k}
is exact.
Recall from Lemma
3.6
the
\dot{U}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}-\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
bimodule
E^{\prime}
such that
E^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}\bullet
is an equivalence of categories. Write
G_{M}:=E^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}}F_{M}
so that the following diagram commutes:

(4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Mod}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet{\otimes}_{\dot{U}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}}G_{M}}

Since
\bullet{\otimes}_{\dot{U}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]}G_{M}
is right exact, the commutativity of Diagram
4
implies that
\bullet\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{M}
is right exact as well.
By Lemma
3.6
,
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2},rat}}
is semi-simple, so all its modules are projective and thus flat. In particular
G_{M}
is flat when we work over
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
so the functor
\bullet{\otimes}_{\dot{U}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]}G_{M}
is exact and the commutativity of Diagram
4
implies that
\bullet\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{M}
is exact as well.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

It was conjectured in
[
GJS
, Remark
2.21
]
and proved in
[
Hai22
, Theorem
1.1
]
that
G_{M}
is isomorphic to the stated skein module
\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
.

Proof of Theorem
4.16
.

By Lemma
4.21
, the submodule of coinvariant vectors of
F_{\mathbb{A}}(n)=\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{<n>}
is the submodule
F^{(0)}_{\mathbb{A}}(n)\subset F_{\mathbb{A}}(n)
spanned by
[T,s^{L}]
where
T\cap a_{L}=\emptyset
. Let
F^{(0)}_{\mathbb{A}}:\operatorname{TL}^{op}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{k}
the associated functor and
i:F^{(0)}_{\mathbb{A}}\hookrightarrow F_{\mathbb{A}}
the inclusion morphism.
We have a left exact sequence in
\widehat{\operatorname{TL}}
:

0\to F_{\mathbb{A}}^{(0)}\xrightarrow{i}F_{\mathbb{A}}\xrightarrow{\Delta^{L}-\eta\otimes id}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\otimes F_{\mathbb{A}}.

By tensoring with
F_{\mathbf{M}}
we get a sequence:

0\to F_{\mathbb{A}}^{(0)}\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{\mathbf{M}}\xrightarrow{i\otimes id}F_{\mathbb{A}}\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{\mathbf{M}}\xrightarrow{(\Delta^{L}-\eta\otimes id)\otimes id}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\otimes F_{\mathbb{A}}\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{\mathbf{M}}.

which is exact while working over
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
and only right exact while working over
k_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
by Lemma
4.23
.
Define an isomorphism
f:F_{\mathbb{A}}\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{\mathbf{M}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
as follows. For
[T,s^{L}]\in F_{\mathbb{A}}(n)
and
T^{\prime}\in F_{\mathbf{M}}(n)
, set
f\left([[T,s^{L}]\otimes[T^{\prime}]]\right):=[T\cup T^{\prime},s^{L}]
where
T\cup T^{\prime}
is the tangle obtained by gluing
T
to
T^{\prime}
while gluing
a_{R}
to
\mathbb{D}_{M}
. The inverse morphism
f^{-1}
is defined by splitting stated tangles in the same manner than in the definition of
\theta_{a_{R}\#a_{L}}
. Define an isomorphism
f_{0}:F_{\mathbb{A}}^{(0)}\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{\mathbf{M}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{q}(M)
in the same manner. The commutativity of the following diagram is a straightforward consequence of the definitions:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathbb{A}}^{(0)}\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{\mathbf{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathbb{A}}\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{\mathbf{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\otimes F_{\mathbb{A}}\otimes_{\operatorname{TL}}F_{\mathbf{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{(rat)}_{q}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{(rat)}_{q}(\mathbf{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}^{(rat)}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\otimes\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Delta^{L}-\eta\otimes id)\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{L}-\eta\otimes id}

So the exactness of the first line when working over
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
(resp. the right exactness of the first line when working over
k_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
) implies the exactness (resp. right exactness) of the second which concludes the proof.

∎

Remark 4.25
.

Identifying locally finite
U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
modules with
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
-comodules, the
n+1
dimensional irreducible representation
V_{n}
of
U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
(so
V_{1}=V
in our notations) corresponds to the comodule
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{(n)}
. It is easy to see that the quotient
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]}
of

{\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]}
by the ideal generated by the

is isomorphic to the limit
\lim_{n\geq 0}(\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{(1)})^{\otimes n}
and the equality
\operatorname{Gr}(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]})=\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{O}_{q}[\mathbb{A}]^{(n)}
in Lemma
4.21
can be reinterpreted dually in terms of
U_{q}\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-modules, by the equality in
K^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}})
:

[(V_{1})^{\otimes n}]=[V_{n}]+\mbox{lower terms},

where ”lower terms” is a linear combinations of
[V_{i}]
with
i<n
. The latter equality comes from the fact that
[V_{n}]=S_{n}([V_{1}])
in
K^{0}(\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}})
(by the quantum Clebsch-Gordan formula) and that the
n
-th Chebyshev polynomial of second species satisfies
S_{n}(X)=X^{n}+\mbox{lower terms}
.

4.5.
Spherical boundary component

Recall that
k_{G}=\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/n}]
for
n=n_{G}
and consider the field of fractions
K_{G}:=\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/n})
and the
K_{G}
vector spaces
\mathcal{S}_{q}^{rat}(\mathbf{M}):=\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})\otimes_{k_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
and
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G,rat}(\mathbf{M}):=\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M})\otimes_{k_{G}}K_{G}
. They both admit a comodule structure over
\mathcal{O}_{q}G^{rat}:=\mathcal{O}_{q}G\otimes_{k_{G}}K_{G}
.
The goal of this subsection is to prove the

Theorem 4.26
.

Let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
be such that the connected component of
\partial M
containing the boundary disc
\mathbb{D}_{M}
is a sphere. Then every element of
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G,rat}(\mathbf{M})
are coinvariant, i.e.
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G,rat}(\mathbf{M})=\operatorname{Char}_{q}^{G,rat}(\mathbf{M})
. Similarly, every element of
\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
are coinvariant, i.e.
\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(\mathbf{M})=\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(\mathbf{M})^{coinv}
.

Together with Theorem
4.16
this implies the

Corollary 4.27
.

Let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
be such that the connected component of
\partial M
containing the boundary disc
\mathbb{D}_{M}
is a sphere. Then the usual (non stated) skein module of
M
over
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
is equal to the stated skein module of
\mathbf{M}
over
K_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
, i.e. the inclusion
i_{*}:\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(M)\to\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
is an isomorphism.

Theorem
4.26
and its proof are very similar and largely inspired (though different) from the work of Gunningham-Jordan-Safronov in
[
GJS
, Corollary
4.2
]
however our argument, illustrated in Figure
15
, is more topological and arguably more enlightening (in
[
GJS
]
no marked
3
-manifold with spherical boundary component is mentioned).
As explained in Remark
4.29
, Theorem
4.26
would not hold if we were working over the ring
k_{G}
instead of
K_{G}
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G,rat}=\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}\otimes_{k_{G}}K_{G}
the category of finite dimensional
\mathcal{O}_{q}G^{rat}
comodules and
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G,rat}}:=\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}}\otimes_{k_{G}}K_{G}
the category of possibly infinite dimensional comodules. The heart of the proof and the reason why we need to work over the field
K_{G}
is the fact that the Müger center of
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G,rat}}
is trivial whereas the Müger center of
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}}
is not.

Throughout this subsection, we fix
\mathbf{M}
satisfying the hypothesis of Theorem
4.26
. Recall that in the braided quantum group
B_{q}G:=\int^{W\in\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}}W^{*}\otimes W\in\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}}
, the counit
\epsilon:B_{q}G\to k
is defined by
\epsilon([w^{*}\otimes w]):=\overrightarrow{ev}_{W}(w^{*}\otimes w)
. For
V,W\in\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}}
,
consider the map

\tau_{V,W}:=V\otimes W^{*}\otimes W\to W^{*}\otimes W\otimes V,\quad\tau_{V,W}:=(id_{W^{*}}\otimes c_{V,W})(c_{W^{*},V}^{-1}\otimes id_{W})=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{field_goal.eps}}}}}}}$}.

Then taking the colimit over
W\in\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}
, one gets a morphism
\tau_{V}:V\otimes B_{q}G\to B_{q}G\otimes V
named the
field goal transformed
by Lyubashenko (see
[
Lyu95
]
). The following lemma is essentially a reformulation of
[
GJS
, Corollary
1.28
, Proposition
1.37
]
. We add the (very simple) proof for the reader convenience.

Lemma 4.28

(
[
GJS
]
)
.

Suppose that
V\in\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G,rat}}
satisfies

\epsilon(x)v=(\epsilon\otimes id_{V})\circ\tau_{V}(v\otimes x),\quad\mbox{ for all }x\in B_{q}G,v\in V,

then
V
is a direct sum of some copies of the trivial comodule
\mathds{1}\in\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G,rat}}
.

Proof.

Let us show that
V
belongs to the Müger center
\mathcal{Z}^{M\ddot{u}ger}
of
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G,rat}}
:

\displaystyle V\in\mathcal{Z}^{M\ddot{u}ger}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow c_{W,V}\circ c_{V,W}=id_{V\otimes W},\quad\mbox{ for all }W\in\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G},

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(id_{V}\otimes\overrightarrow{ev}_{W})\circ(c_{W,V}c_{V,W}\otimes id_{W^{*}})=(id_{V}\otimes\overrightarrow{ev}_{W}),\quad\mbox{ for all }W\in\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G},

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(\overrightarrow{ev}_{W}\otimes id_{V})\tau_{V,W}=id_{V}\otimes\overrightarrow{ev}_{W},\quad\mbox{ for all }W\in\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G},

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(id_{V}\otimes\epsilon)=(\epsilon\otimes id_{V})\circ\tau_{V}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\epsilon(x)v=(\epsilon\otimes id_{V})\circ\tau_{V}(v\otimes x),\quad\mbox{ for all }x\in B_{q}G,v\in V.

We conclude using the fact that any element in
\mathcal{Z}^{M\ddot{u}ger}
is a trivial comodule.

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.26
.

Write
V=\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G,rat}(\mathbf{M})
and let us prove that it satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
4.28
. The proof is illustrated in Figure
15
. Let
x\in B_{q}G
and
v\in\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G,rat}(\mathbf{M})
be an element of the form
v:=[y\otimes\alpha]
with
\alpha\in P_{n}(M)
a
n
-bottom tangle and
y\in(B_{q}G)^{\otimes n}
. Let us prove that
\epsilon(x)v=(\epsilon\otimes id_{V})\circ\tau_{V}(v\otimes x)
. Let
\beta_{0}\in P_{1}(M)
be the trivial
1
-bottom tangle made of an arc lying in a small neighborhood of
\mathbb{D}_{M}
. More precisely, if
\bm{\eta}\in BT(1,0)
is the counit of Figure
5
and
\eta_{M}
is the only element of
P_{0}(M)
then
\beta_{0}:=\eta_{M}\circ\bm{\eta}
. Let
\beta_{0}\cup\alpha\in P_{n+1}(M)
be the
n+1
bottom tangle obtained by stacking
\beta_{0}
on top (in the height direction) of
\alpha
. Similarly, let
\alpha\cup\beta_{0}\in P_{n+1}(M)
be the bottom tangle obtained by stacking
\beta_{0}
on the bottom of
\alpha
. In more precise terms, we set

\beta_{0}\cup\alpha:=\alpha\circ(\bm{\eta}\wedge\mathds{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathds{1}),\quad\alpha\cup\beta_{0}:=\alpha\circ(\mathds{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathds{1}\wedge\bm{\eta}).

So by definition of the counit
\epsilon
, one has

\epsilon(x)v=[(x\otimes y)\otimes(\beta_{0}\cup\alpha)]=[(y\otimes x)\otimes(\alpha\cup\beta_{0})]\in\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}).

Let

T:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{BT_field_goal.eps}}}}}}}$}\in\mathfrak{bt}(n+1,n+1).

Then by definition of the field goal transform, one has

(\epsilon\otimes id_{V})\circ\tau_{V}(v\otimes x)=[(x\otimes y)\otimes\left((\alpha\cup\beta_{0})\cdot T\right)]\in\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G,rat}(\mathbf{M}).

The equality
\epsilon(x)v=(\epsilon\otimes id_{V})\circ\tau_{V}(v\otimes x)
follows from the fact, illustrated in Figure
15
, that the
n+1
bottom tangles
\beta_{0}\cup\alpha
and
(\alpha\cup\beta_{0})\cdot T
are isotopic in
\mathbf{M}
because the boundary component containing
\mathbb{D}_{M}
is a sphere. We conclude using Lemma
4.28
.

Figure 15.
An illustration of the isotopy
\beta_{0}\cup\alpha\sim(\alpha\cup\beta_{0})\cdot T
.

∎

Remark 4.29
.

Note that in Theorem
4.26
, it is important to work over the field
K_{G}
instead of the ring
k_{G}
. Indeed, as we shall prove in Section
5
, the module
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})\otimes_{q^{1/4}=1}\mathbb{Z}
, obtained by setting
q^{1/4}=1
, is isomorphic as an
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
-comodule to the ring of regular functions of the representation scheme with coaction given by conjugacy. Since the action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
on the space
\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{1}(M),\operatorname{SL}_{2})
by conjugacy is not trivial, this implies that
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
does not have a trivial coaction, i.e. that the analogue of Theorem
4.26
over
k_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}
does not hold. Let us illustrate this phenomenon on a concrete example. Let
\alpha
be a non contractible
1
-bottom tangle in
\mathbf{M}
and for
i,j=\pm
let
\alpha_{ij}\in\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
denote the class of the arc
\alpha
with state
v_{i}
at its higher endpoint and
v_{j}
at its lower endpoint. Let
\alpha^{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
be the class of the closed arc obtained from
\alpha
by gluing its two endpoints together in the interior of
M
.
Write
\begin{pmatrix}D_{++}&D_{+-}\\
D_{-+}&D_{--}\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-A^{5/2}\\
A^{1/2}&0\end{pmatrix}
the matrix coefficients of the half-twist.
A simple skein computation illustrated in Figure
16
shows that in
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
we have the equality in
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
:

(5)

(q^{4}-1)\alpha_{ij}=q(1-q^{2})D_{ij}\alpha^{0}.

In particular
\alpha_{++}\in\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
is not a coinvariant vector, but since
(q^{4}-1)\alpha_{++}=0
, its class in
\mathcal{S}_{q}^{rat}(\mathbf{M})
vanishes. In general, Equation (
5
) shows that the class of any stated arc
\alpha_{ij}
in
\mathcal{S}_{q}^{rat}(\mathbf{M})
is proportional to the coinvariant vector
\alpha^{0}
, so is coinvariant. The proof of Theorem
4.26
shows that
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})
belongs to the Müger center of
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}}
even though it is not a trivial comodule because of the existence of torsion elements which are not coinvariant.

Figure 16.
An illustration of Equation (
5
). The isotopy
\sim
is similar to the isotopy of Figure
15
. The second equality is obtained by resolving the crossings using the Kauffman-bracket skein relations.

5.
Classical limit

Let
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{M}):=\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})\otimes_{q^{1/4}=1}\mathbb{Z}
. The goal of this section is to endow
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{M})
with a ring structure and to prove that it is isomorphic to the ring of regular functions of the representation scheme
\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{M})
.

5.1.
Ring structure on
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{M})

Let
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]:=\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]\otimes_{q^{1/4}=1}\mathbb{Z}
: it is the classical integral form of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{+1}:\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}\to\operatorname{RComod}_{\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]}
be the composition of
\mathcal{S}_{q}
with the change of scalars
\bullet\otimes_{q^{1/4}=1}\mathbb{Z}:\operatorname{Mod}_{k}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Lemma 5.1
.

\mathcal{S}_{+1}
is the left Kan extension of
\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=1.98335pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=1.98335pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=1.55836pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.07782pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}}}
along
\iota:\operatorname{BT}\to\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
.

Proof.

The lemma follows from Lemma
3.2
together with the fact that the functor
\bullet\otimes_{q^{1/4}=1}\mathbb{Z}
has right adjoint
\operatorname{Hom}_{k}(\cdot,\mathbb{Z})
.
∎

.

Definition 5.2
.

Equip

with the product defined on two classes of stated tangles

by first isotoping
T_{1}
and

such that they do not intersect and such that the heights of
\partial T_{1}
are bigger than the heights of
\partial T_{2}
and then defining

[T_{1},s_{1}]\cdot[T_{2},s_{2}]:=[T_{1}\cup T_{2},(s_{1},s_{2})]
.

The transparence skein relation

(proved in

of a stated tangle does not change if we change the height order of the elements of

. In particular
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{M})
is a commutative ring.

Remark 5.3
.

If we had chosen
q^{1/4}=-1
, we still would have obtained a commutative ring; it is not difficult to prove that this ring is (non canonically) isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{M})
. However for the more standard choice
q^{1/4}=\sqrt{-1}
, for which
A=-1
, the product is still defined but the stated skein module is no longer commutative (unlike the usual skein module) because the height exchange relation
