and

: so
\pi_{M}^{fr}
is a quotient of
P_{M}
and we have quotient maps (of left
\operatorname{BT}
-modules)
P_{M}\to\pi_{M}^{fr}\to\pi_{M}
. The isomorphism of Lemma
5.1
can rewritten as

g_{M}:\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{M})\cong\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi^{fr}_{M}].

In order to prove Theorem
5.8
, we are thus reduced to construct an isomorphism

\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi^{fr}_{M}]\cong\mathchoice{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=4.20001pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{0}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{M}].

By precomposing the functor
\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}:\operatorname{BT}_{1}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}
with
s:\operatorname{BT}_{0}\to\operatorname{BT}_{1}
, we can see
\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}
as a right
\operatorname{BT}_{0}
module. We now define an explicit isomorphism between this right
\operatorname{BT}_{0}
-module and
\mathchoice{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=4.20001pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}
.

Let
\beta_{i}\in P_{1}(\mathbf{H}_{n})
be the oriented bottom tangle depicted by
\beta_{i}:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{BT_Ti.eps}}}}}}}$}
.

Lemma 5.12
.

For each
n\geq 0
, one has isomorphisms
\omega_{n}:\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{H}_{n})]\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{n})
of
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
comodules algebras characterized by the formula

\omega_{n}\begin{pmatrix}X^{\beta_{i}}_{++}&X^{\beta_{i}}_{+-}\\
X^{\beta_{i}}_{-+}&X^{\beta_{i}}_{--}\end{pmatrix}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\beta_{i}}_{++}&{\beta_{i}}_{+-}\\
{\beta_{i}}_{-+}&{\beta_{i}}_{--}\end{pmatrix}.

Moreover, for
\mu_{0}\in\operatorname{BT}_{0}(a,b)
then the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{H}_{a})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{H}_{b})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{a})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{b})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mu_{0})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s(\mu_{0})_{*}}

Proof.

The fact that the
\omega_{n}
are isomorphisms of rings and of
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
-comodules is a particular (easy) case of
[
KQ19
, Theorem
3.17
]
(see also
[
Kor20
, Theorem
4.7
]
). By Theorem
A.1
, every morphism in
\operatorname{BT}_{0}
is obtained by composition and tensoring the generating morphisms
(\mu,\eta,\Delta,\epsilon,S^{\pm 1},\theta^{\pm 1})
of Figure
5
. So to prove the naturality of
\omega
, we need to prove the commutativity of the diagram in the particular cases where
s(\mu_{0})
is one of these morphisms. For
(\mu,\eta)
this follows from the fact that
\omega_{n}
are algebras morphisms. For
\Delta,\epsilon,S^{\pm 1}
this follows from the computations made in the proof of Theorem
4.10
to prove that
f
is a morphism of braided Hopf algebra object. For
\theta^{\pm 1}
this is obvious. This completes the proof.

∎

Recall from Notations
5.11
the group
G_{a}\subset\operatorname{BT}_{1}(a,a)
generated by the involutive elements
\theta_{i}^{a}
. Let us describe the right action of
G_{a}
on
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{a})
. First define
\Theta\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}])
the automorphism sending the generator
x_{ij}
for
i,j=\pm
to
-x_{ij}
. Recall that as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module one has
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{a})\cong\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{1})^{\otimes a}\cong(\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}])^{\otimes a}
and define
\Theta_{i}^{a}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{a}))
by
\Theta_{i}^{a}=id^{\otimes i-1}\otimes\Theta\otimes id^{\otimes a-i}
. Said differently, the automorphism
\Theta_{i}^{a}
sends each generator
(\beta_{i})_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
to
-(\beta_{i})_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
and each
(\beta_{j})_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
with
j\neq i
to
+(\beta_{j})_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
. In general, for
g=\theta_{1}^{n_{1}}\ldots\theta_{a}^{n_{a}}\in G_{a}
, we write
\Theta_{g}:=(\Theta_{1}^{a})^{n_{1}}\ldots(\Theta_{a}^{a})^{n_{a}}
.

Lemma 5.13
.

For
g\in G_{a}
and
x\in\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{a})
, one has
x\cdot g=\Theta_{g}(x)
, where
x\cdot g
denotes the right action coming from the functoriality of
\mathcal{S}_{+1}
.

Proof.

We need to prove that for each generator
(\beta_{i})_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
of
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{a})
and for each generator
\theta_{j}^{a}
of
G_{a}
, one has
(\beta_{i})_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\cdot\theta_{i}^{a}=(-1)^{\delta_{i,j}}(\beta_{i})_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
. This follows from the skein relation

in
\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{a})
.

∎

Note that the left
\operatorname{BT}
module
\eta
of Definition
5.4
passes to the quotient to a left
\operatorname{BT}_{1}
module and that
a spin function is, by definition, a morphism
w:\pi_{M}^{fr}\to\eta
of left
\operatorname{BT}_{1}
modules.

Let
[\beta_{i}]\in\mathrm{H}_{1}(\mathbf{H}_{a};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
be the homology class of the closed curve obtained from
\beta_{i}
by joining its endpoints, i.e. the simple closed curve encircling the
i
-th hole of the punctured disc
\mathbb{D}_{a}
. Let
\varphi_{i}\in\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbf{H}_{a};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
be the dual element sending
[\beta_{j}]
to
\delta_{ij}
.
Consider the isomorphism
G_{a}\cong\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbf{H}_{a};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
sending
\theta_{i}^{a}
to
\varphi_{i}
. Using this isomorphism, for
\alpha\in\pi_{M}^{fr}(\mathbf{H}_{a})
, we can consider the automorphism
\Theta_{w_{1}(\alpha)}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{a}))
and the group element
\theta_{w_{1}(\alpha)}\in G_{a}
.

Proof of Theorem
5.8
.

Let us define an isomorphism

f:\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi^{fr}_{M}]\cong\mathchoice{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=4.20001pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{0}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{M}].

Identify the tensor products as

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi^{fr}_{M}]={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left(\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{n})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi^{fr}_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})]\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{I}_{1}$}\right.},

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=4.20001pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{0}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{M}]={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left(\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{H}_{n})]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})]\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{I}_{0}$}\right.},

where
\mathcal{I}_{i}
is spanned by elements of the form
u\cdot\mu\otimes v-u\otimes\mu\cdot v
for
\mu
a morphism in
\operatorname{BT}_{i}
. Let us slightly re-write the first quotient as

\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi^{fr}_{M}]={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\left(\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{n})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[G_{n}]}\mathbb{Z}[\pi^{fr}_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})]\right)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{1}$}\right.}

where
\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\prime}
is spanned by elements
u\cdot\mu\otimes v-u\otimes\mu\cdot v
where
\mu
is a morphism in
\operatorname{BT}_{1}
such that
s(\mu)=\mu
.

For
n\geq 0
, we define an isomorphism

f_{n}:\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{n})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[G_{n}]}\mathbb{Z}[\pi^{fr}_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})]\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{H}_{n})]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})]

by

f_{n}(x\otimes y):=\omega_{n}^{-1}(x\cdot w_{n}(y))\otimes p(y).

Here
p:\pi^{fr}_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})\to\pi_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})
is the quotient map and
w_{n}:\pi_{M}^{fr}(\mathbf{H}_{n})\to\mathrm{H}^{1}(\mathbf{H}_{a};\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})\cong G_{n}
is the spin function.
The fact that
f_{n}
is well-defined, i.e. that
f_{n}(x\cdot g\otimes y)=f_{n}(x\otimes g\cdot y)
for all
g\in G_{n}
, comes from the naturality of
w_{n}
with respect to the morphisms in
G_{n}
together with the fact that
w_{1}\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{BT_Theta.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)=1
.
The fact that
f_{n}
is an isomorphism comes from the fact that
\omega_{n}
is an isomorphism together with the fact that
\pi_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\pi_{M}^{fr}(\mathbf{H}_{n})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$G_{n}$}\right.}
. The fact that
f_{n}
is
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
equivariant follows from the fact that
\omega_{n}
is equivariant.

Let
\widetilde{f}:=\oplus_{n\geq 0}f_{n}
and let us prove that
\widetilde{f}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{1})=\mathcal{I}_{0}
. Consider an element
X=u\cdot\mu\otimes v-u\otimes\mu\cdot v\in\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{1}
with
\mu\in\operatorname{BT}_{1}(a,b)
and
u\in\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{H}_{b})
,
v\in\pi_{M}^{fr}(\mathbf{H}_{a})
. Then

\displaystyle f_{a}(u\cdot\mu\otimes v)

\displaystyle:=\omega_{a}^{-1}(u\cdot\mu\cdot\theta_{w_{a}(v)})\otimes p(v)

\displaystyle=\omega_{a}^{-1}(u\cdot\theta_{w_{b}(\mu\cdot v)}\cdot\mu)\otimes p(v)

\displaystyle\mbox{ by naturality of }w

\displaystyle=\omega_{a}^{-1}(u\cdot\theta_{w_{b}(\mu\cdot v)})\cdot\mu\otimes p(v)

\displaystyle\mbox{ by Lemma \ref{lemma_classical1} and the fact that }s(\mu)=\mu.

Thus, writing
x:=u\cdot\theta_{w_{b}(\mu\cdot v)}
and
y:=p(v)
one has

f_{a}(u\cdot\mu\otimes v)-f_{b}(u\otimes\mu\cdot v)=x\cdot\mu\otimes y-x\otimes\mu\cdot y\in\mathcal{I}_{0}.

Therefore, we have proved the inclusion
\widetilde{f}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{1})\subset\mathcal{I}_{0}
. To prove the reverse inclusion
\mathcal{I}_{0}\subset\widetilde{f}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{1})
, consider
x\in\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{H}_{b})]
,
y\in\pi_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{a})
and
\mu_{0}\in\operatorname{BT}_{0}(a,b)
and consider the generator
X:=x\cdot\mu_{0}\otimes y-x\otimes\mu_{0}\cdot y\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
. Let
\mu\in\operatorname{BT}_{1}(a,b)
be a lift of
\mu_{0}
such that
s(\mu)=\mu
and choose
v\in\pi_{M}^{fr}(\mathbf{H}_{a})
a lift of
y
such that
w_{a}(v)=0
. Set
u:=\omega_{b}(x)
and
Y:=u\cdot\mu\otimes v-u\otimes\mu\cdot v\in\mathcal{I}_{1}^{\prime}
. Then by definition one has
\widetilde{f}(Y)=X
so
\mathcal{I}_{0}\subset\widetilde{f}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{1})
. We thus have proved that
\widetilde{f}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}_{1})=\mathcal{I}_{0}
so the isomorphism
\widetilde{f}
induces an isomorphism

f:\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi^{fr}_{M}]\cong\mathchoice{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=6.00002pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=4.20001pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}{{\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}]\,\smash{\vrule height=3.0pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{0}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{0}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{M}]

which is
\mathcal{O}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
-equivariant since
\widetilde{f}
is equivariant as well.
Define the isomorphism
\eta_{w}^{-1}:\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{M})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{M})]
as the composition

\eta_{w}^{-1}:\mathcal{S}_{+1}(\mathbf{M})\xrightarrow{g_{M}}\mathchoice{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.46666pt,depth=2.12502pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.82668pt,depth=2.125pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}{{\mathcal{S}_{+1}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.71904pt,depth=2.70009pt}}_{\,\operatorname{BT}_{1}}}\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}_{1}}\mathbb{Z}[\pi^{fr}_{M}]\xrightarrow{f}\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{M})]\xrightarrow{\kappa_{M}^{-1}}\mathcal{O}[\mathcal{R}_{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{M})].

That
\eta_{w}
is equivariant follows from the fact that each above map is equivariant. It remains to prove the explicit formula for a stated arc given in Theorem
5.8
. Write
C_{++}=C_{--}:=0
,
C_{-+}:=-C_{+-}=1
so that the formula we need to prove writes
\eta_{w}(X_{ij}^{\gamma})=(-1)^{w_{1}(\gamma)}\sum_{k=\pm}C_{ik}\gamma_{kj}
. The arc
\gamma
defines a
1
-bottom tangles
\gamma\in P_{1}(M)
and we denote by
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}
its images in the quotients
\pi_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
and
\pi_{M}^{fr}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
respectively. Then
\kappa_{M}
(defined in the proof of Lemma
5.7
) sends
X_{ij}^{\gamma}
to the class
[X^{\beta_{1}}_{ij}\otimes\gamma_{0}]
. The isomorphism
g_{M}
(defined from the isomorphism
G^{-1}
in the proof of Theorem
4.14
by tensoring by
\mathbb{Z}
) sends a stated arc
\gamma_{ab}
to the class
[(\beta_{1})_{ab}\otimes\gamma_{1}]
. Now the isomorphism
\omega_{1}
in Lemma
5.12
reads
\omega_{1}(X^{\beta_{1}}_{ij})=\sum_{k}C_{ik}(\beta_{1})_{kj}
so the desired formula follows from the equalities

\eta_{M}(X_{ij}^{\gamma})=g_{M}^{-1}\circ f\circ\kappa_{M}(X_{ij}^{\gamma})=g_{M}^{-1}\circ f([X^{\beta_{1}}_{ij}\otimes\gamma_{0}])=(-1)^{w_{1}(\gamma)}g_{M}^{-1}(\sum_{k=\pm}C_{ik}[(\beta_{1})_{kj}\otimes\gamma_{1}])=(-1)^{w_{1}(\gamma)}\sum_{k=\pm}C_{ik}\gamma_{kj},

where we used that
(\beta_{1})_{kj}\cdot\theta_{w_{1}(\gamma)}=\Theta_{w_{1}(\gamma)}((\beta_{1})_{kj})=(-1)^{w_{1}(\gamma)}(\beta_{1})_{kj}
as proved in Lemma
5.13
. This concludes the proof.

∎

6.
Quantum Van Kampen theorems

6.1.
Quantum Van Kampen for quantum fundamental groups

Recall that
\mathbb{D}^{2}
is the unit disc of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
h:\mathbb{D}^{2}\to[-1,1]
is the projection on the
y
axis and let
\partial_{+}\mathbb{D},\partial_{-}\mathbb{D}\subset\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}
and
\mathbb{D}^{+},\mathbb{D}^{-}\subset\mathbb{D}^{2}
be the subsets of points
p
for which
h(p)\geq 0
and
h(p)\leq 0
respectively. For
\mathbf{M}=(M,\iota_{M})\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
, we write
\partial_{\pm}\mathbb{D}_{M}:=\iota_{M}(\partial_{\pm}\mathbb{D})
and
\mathbb{D}_{M}^{\pm}:=\iota_{M}(\mathbb{D}^{\pm})
.

Let
\mathbf{M}_{1},\mathbf{M}_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
,
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma,a)\in\operatorname{MS}
a connected marked surface with a single boundary arc
a
. Consider oriented embeddings
\phi_{1}:\overline{\Sigma}\hookrightarrow\partial M_{1}
and
\phi_{2}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow\partial M_{2}
sending
a
to
\partial_{-}\mathbb{D}_{M_{1}}
and
\partial_{+}\mathbb{D}_{M_{2}}
respectively. Here
\overline{\Sigma}
is
\Sigma
with the opposite orientation.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
be the marked
3
-manifold where

M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$M_{1}\bigsqcup M_{2}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(\phi_{1}(p)\sim\phi_{2}(p),p\in\Sigma)$}\right.}

and the boundary disc
\mathbb{D}_{M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}}
is obtained by gluing
\mathbb{D}_{M_{1}}
with
\mathbb{D}_{M_{2}}
by identifying
\phi_{1}(a)=\partial_{-}\mathbb{D}_{M_{1}}
with
\phi_{2}(a)=\partial_{+}\mathbb{D}_{M_{2}}
.

By construction, there is a natural projection map
\pi^{0}:\mathbf{M}_{1}\bigsqcup\mathbf{M}_{2}\to\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2}
. Since we prefer to work in the monoidal category
(\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}},\wedge)
, let us define a morphism
\pi:\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2}\to\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\bigsqcup M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

commutes up to isotopy (i.e. such that
\pi^{0}
is isotopic to
\pi\circ\iota
), where
\iota
is the natural inclusion. Recall that
\mathbb{T}
is a ball
\mathbb{B}^{3}
with three boundary discs and that
M_{1}\wedge M_{2}
is obtained from
M_{1}\bigsqcup M_{2}\bigsqcup\mathbb{T}
by gluing
\mathbb{D}_{M_{1}}
to one disc, say
e_{1}
of
\mathbb{T}
and by gluing
\mathbb{D}_{2}
to another disc, say
e_{2}
of
\mathbb{T}
. The closed intervals
\partial_{-}\mathbb{D}_{M_{1}}
and
\partial_{+}\mathbb{D}_{M_{2}}
are glued along closed intervals
\partial_{-}e_{1}
and
\partial_{+}e_{2}
respectively. Isotope
e_{2}
to a disc
e_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\partial_{-}e_{1}
coincides with
\partial_{+}e_{2}^{\prime}
and let
M^{\prime}
be the
3
-manifold obtained by gluing
M_{1}
to
e_{1}
and
M_{2}
to
e_{2}^{\prime}
as in Figure
17
, so that
M^{\prime}
is isotopic to
M_{1}\wedge M_{2}
. The manifold
M:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$M^{\prime}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(\phi_{1}(p)\sim\phi_{2}(p),p\in\Sigma)$}\right.}
is isotopic to
M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}
so the quotient map

M^{\prime}\to M
defines a surjective morphism
\pi:\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2}\to\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2}
and clearly
\pi^{0}
is isotopic to
\pi\circ\iota
.

Definition 6.2
.

The morphism

\pi:\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2}\to\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2}
is called the
gluing morphism
.

Figure 17.
An illustration of the gluing morphism
\pi:\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2}\to\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

The gluing operation is associative up to isotopy, i.e. one has
(\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2})\cup_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\mathbf{M}_{3}=\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{M}_{2}\cup_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\mathbf{M}_{3})
and if
f_{(12)}:\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2}\to\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2}
,
f_{(23)}:\mathbf{M}_{2}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{3}\to\mathbf{M}_{2}\cup_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\mathbf{M}_{3}
,

f_{(12,3)}:(\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2})\wedge\mathbf{M}_{3}\to\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2}\cup_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\mathbf{M}_{3}

and
f_{(1,23)}:\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge(\mathbf{M}_{2}\cup_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\mathbf{M}_{3})\to\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2}\cup_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\mathbf{M}_{3}

are the four gluing morphisms, then
f_{(12,3)}\circ(f_{(12)}\wedge id_{M_{3}})
is isotopic to
f_{(1,23)}\circ(id_{M_{1}}\wedge f_{(23)})
.

Proof.

The lemma is a straightforward consequence of the definitions.
∎

By replacing
\mathbf{M}_{1}
and
\mathbf{M}_{2}
by
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
in the preceding discussion, while gluing
\Sigma\times\{-1\}
of the first copy to
\Sigma\times\{+1\}
in the second copy, we get a gluing morphism

\mu:(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\wedge(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\to\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
which endows
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
with a structure of algebra in
(\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}},\wedge)
. Similarly, by gluing
M_{1}
to
\Sigma\times I
while identifying
\phi_{1}(p)\sim(p,+1)
for
p\in\Sigma
, we obtain a gluing morphism
\nabla_{1}:\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I\to\mathbf{M}_{1}
turning
\mathbf{M}_{1}
into a right
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
module in

(\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}},\wedge)
. Similarly, by gluing
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
to
\mathbf{M}_{2}
while identifying
\phi_{2}(p)\sim(p,-1)
, we get a gluing morphism
\nabla_{2}:\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2}\to\mathbf{M}_{2}
turning
\mathbf{M}_{2}
into a left
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
module. Note that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{1}\wedge id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\wedge\nabla_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

commutes up to isotopy by Lemma
6.3
. By abuse of notations, for
i=1,2
, still denote by
\nabla_{i}
the image of
\nabla_{i}
by the quantum fundamental group functor
P
and let us identify the elements
P_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}\cong P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{D}P_{M_{2}}
and
P_{M_{1}\wedge\Sigma\wedge M_{2}}\cong P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{D}P_{\Sigma}\otimes_{D}P_{M_{2}}
in
\widehat{\operatorname{BT}}
using the isomorphism of (the proof of) Lemma
3.11
.

Definition 6.4
.

Let

P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{P_{\Sigma}}P_{M_{2}}\in\widehat{\operatorname{BT}}
be the coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{D}P_{\Sigma}\otimes_{D}P_{M_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{D}P_{M_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{P_{\Sigma}}P_{M_{2}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{1}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\nabla_{2}}

Since the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{D}P_{\Sigma}\otimes_{D}P_{M_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{D}P_{M_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{1}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\nabla_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\pi)}

commutes, there exists a unique morphism
\kappa:P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{P_{\Sigma}}P_{M_{2}}\to P_{M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}}
making commuting the obvious diagram.

The following was proposed without proof by Habiro in
[
Hab12
]
:

Theorem 6.5
.

(Quantum Van Kampen theorem for quantum fundamental groups)

The morphism
\kappa:P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{P_{\Sigma}}P_{M_{2}}\to P_{M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}}
is an isomorphism.

The proof of Theorem
6.5
is the most technical part of the paper and will be cut into several lemmas.
Let us first introduce some terminology. Write
M:=M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}
for simplicity. Consider the embedding
\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}:M_{1}\wedge M_{2}\hookrightarrow M
defined as the composition

\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}:M_{1}\wedge M_{2}\subset M_{1}\wedge(\Sigma\times I)\wedge M_{2}\xrightarrow{\pi\circ(\nabla_{1}\wedge id)}M.

Here
M_{1}\wedge(\Sigma\times I)\wedge M_{2}
is, by definition, a ball
\mathbb{B}^{3}
with three discs on the boundary to which we have attached
M_{1},M_{2},\Sigma\times I
and we see
M_{1}\wedge M_{2}\subset M_{1}\wedge(\Sigma\times I)\wedge M_{2}
as the union of
\mathbb{B}^{3}
with
M_{1},M_{2}
only. Define also an embedding
\iota_{\Sigma}
as the composition:

\iota_{\Sigma}:\Sigma\times I\subset M_{1}\wedge(\Sigma\times I)\wedge M_{2}\xrightarrow{\pi\circ(\nabla_{1}\wedge id)}M.

So
M
is the union
M=\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}(M_{1}\wedge M_{2})\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)
.
Write
\mathbb{B}_{M}:=\pi\circ(\nabla_{1}\wedge id)(\mathbb{B}^{3})
, so
\mathbb{B}_{M}\subset M
is a ball containing the boundary disc
\mathbb{D}_{M}
and contained in the image of
\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}
. We can further decompose
M
as the union
M=\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}(M_{1})\cup\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}(M_{2})\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)\cup\mathbb{B}_{M}
as illustrated in Figure
18
.

Figure 18.
An illustration of the decomposition
M=\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}(M_{1})\cup\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}(M_{2})\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)\cup\mathbb{B}_{M}
.

Let
\overline{M}_{1}:=\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}(M_{1})\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)\cup\mathbb{B}_{M}\subset M
and
\overline{M}_{2}:=\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}(M_{2})\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)\cup\mathbb{B}_{M}\subset M
. So
\overline{M}_{1}\cup\overline{M}_{2}=M
and
\overline{M}_{1}\cap\overline{M}_{2}=\mathbb{B}_{M}\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)
. For
n\geq 0
, by abuse of notations, we denote by
P_{n}(M_{1}\wedge M_{2})
the set of isotopy classes of
n
-bottom tangles in
\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}(M_{1}\wedge M_{2})
, i.e. we now see
M_{1}\wedge M_{2}
as a submanifold of
M
via
\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}
.

Definition 6.6
.

For
n\geq 1
, and
\alpha,\beta\in P_{n}(M_{1}\wedge M_{2})
, write:

(1)

\alpha\sim_{\phi}\beta
if
\alpha
and
\beta
are related by a composition of isotopies, each having support either in
\overline{M}_{1}
or
\overline{M}_{2}
;

(2)

\alpha\sim_{\psi}\beta
if
\alpha
and
\beta
are isotopic in
M
.

Identify the spaces
P_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})
and
P_{n}(M)
using Lemma
2.10
so that
P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{P_{\Sigma}}P_{M_{2}}(\mathbf{H}_{n})
becomes identified with the coequalizer:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{P_{\Sigma}}P_{M_{2}}(\mathbf{H}_{n}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{1}\wedge id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\wedge\nabla_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\pi)}

Here
\nabla_{i}
has image
\overline{M}_{i}
, so we have an isomorphism

(6)

\varphi:P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{P_{\Sigma}}P_{M_{2}}(\mathbf{H}_{n})\cong{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$P_{n}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim_{\phi}$}\right.}.

The embedding
\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}
induces a map
\Psi:=(\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}})^{*}:P_{n}(M_{1}\wedge M_{2})\to P_{n}(M)
such that
\Psi(\alpha)=\Psi(\beta)\Leftrightarrow\alpha\sim_{\psi}\beta
.
Let
\Sigma^{0}:=\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times\{0\})\subset M
and
\Sigma^{0}\times I:=\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)
.
To prove the surjectivity of
\Psi
, we introduce the:

Definition 6.7
.

Let
\alpha
be an
n
bottom tangle in
M_{1}\wedge M_{2}\subset M
which is transverse to
\Sigma^{0}
. Let
v\in\alpha\cap\Sigma^{0}
, so
v=\iota_{\Sigma}(v_{0})
for
v_{0}\in\Sigma
, and suppose that the connected component
\alpha(v)
of
\alpha\cap(\Sigma^{0}\times I)
is the straight line
\iota_{\Sigma}(v_{0}\times I)
with a fixed framing of the form
\iota_{\Sigma}(\overrightarrow{v}_{0}\times I)
for
\overrightarrow{v}_{0}
a fixed vector in the unitary tangent bundle
U_{v_{0}}\Sigma
of
v_{0}
. Let
w\in\mathbb{B}_{M}
and
\gamma:v\to w
a smooth path in
\Sigma_{0}\cup\mathbb{B}_{M}
which only intersects
\alpha
in
v
.

The
push
of
\alpha
along
\gamma
is the bottom tangle
\alpha^{\prime}
obtained from
\alpha
by replacing the framed arc
\alpha(v)
by an arc of the form
\alpha^{\prime}(v)=\gamma^{-1}\gamma
, i.e.
\alpha^{\prime}(v)
goes from
\iota(v_{0},-1)
to
w
along
\gamma
and comes back to
\iota(v_{0},+1)
along
\gamma^{-1}
so that
\alpha^{\prime}(v)
does not intersect
\Sigma_{0}
in Figure
19
. The framing is chosen such that
\alpha(v)
and
\alpha^{\prime}(v)
are isotopic, so are
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
.

Figure 19.
An illustration of the pushing operation.

Lemma 6.8
.

The map
\Psi:P_{n}(M_{1}\wedge M_{2})\to P_{n}(M)
is surjective. Therefore,
\Psi
induces a bijection:

(7)

P_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})\cong{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$P_{n}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2})$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\sim_{\psi}$}\right.}.

Proof.

Starting with a
n
-bottom tangle
\alpha
in
M
, we first isotope it such that it intersects
\Sigma^{0}
transversally as in Definition
6.7
and then push one-by-one each point of
\alpha \cap\Sigma^{0}
inside
\mathbb{B}_{M}
along arbitrary paths in order to get a bottom tangle
\alpha^{\prime}
such that:
(1)

\alpha^{\prime}
is isotopic to
\alpha
and
(2)

\alpha^{\prime}
is in the image of
\iota_{M_{1}\wedge M_{2}}
. This proves the surjectivity.
∎

Obviously,
\alpha\sim_{\phi}\beta
implies
\alpha\sim_{\psi}\beta
so we get a surjective map
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$P_{n}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim_{\phi}$}\right.}\to{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$P_{n}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim_{\psi}$}\right.}
which, using the isomorphisms of Equations (
6
) and (
7
), corresponds the morphism
\kappa:P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{P_{\Sigma}}P_{M_{2}}\to P_{M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}}
of Theorem
6.5
. To prove the injectivity, we need to prove the

Proposition 6.9
.

For
n\geq 1
and
\alpha,\beta\in P_{n}(M_{1}\wedge M_{2})
,
\alpha\sim_{\psi}\beta
implies
\alpha\sim_{\phi}\beta
.

Lemma 6.10
.

If Proposition
6.9
is true for
n=1
, then it is true for every
n\geq 1
.

Proof.

Let
n\geq 2
and
\alpha,\beta\in\bm{P}_{n}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2})
and suppose that
\alpha
and
\beta
are related by an isotopy
H
in
M
. Let
\alpha=\alpha^{(1)}\cup\ldots\cup\alpha^{(n)}
be the
n
components of
\alpha
. Up to changing
H
by an isotopic isotopy, one can suppose that
H
is a composition of isotopies each of which moves one strand while leaving the other
n-1
strands fixed. Therefore, by induction on the number of such isotopies in the decomposition, we are reduced to prove the lemma in the case where
H
leaves
\alpha^{(2)},\ldots,\alpha^{(n)}
fixed whereas it only isotopes the component
\alpha^{(1)}
. Choosing an open tubular neighborhood
N
of
\alpha^{(2)}\cup\ldots\cup\alpha^{(n)}
and replacing
M
by
M^{\prime}=M\setminus N
, the isotopy
H
induces an isotopy
H^{\prime}
between
\alpha^{(1)}
and
\beta^{(1)}
in
\bm{P}_{1}(M^{\prime})
. Since we assume that Proposition
6.9
holds for
n=1
, we can decompose
H^{\prime}
as a composition of isotopies with support in
\overline{M_{1}}\setminus N
and
\overline{M_{2}}\setminus N
. Therefore
\alpha\sim_{\phi}\beta
and this concludes the proof.
∎

We are reduced to prove Proposition
6.9
in the case
n=1
. We will imitate the proof of the classical Van Kampen theorem as it appears in Hatcher’s book
[
Hat00
]
. Fix a Riemannian metric on
M
and let
\pi:UM\to M
be the unitary tangent bundle. A
1
-ribbon tangle
\alpha
can be seen as a smooth map
c_{\alpha}:[0,1]\to UM
such that
(1)

c_{\alpha}
is an embedding,
(2)

c_{\alpha}(0),c_{\alpha}(1)\in\mathbb{D}_{M}
have distinct heights and framings towards the height direction and
(3)
the framing is normal to the direction, i.e. for all
t\in[0,1]
if
v:=\pi\circ c_{\alpha}(t)
then
c_{\alpha}(t)
is orthogonal to
\mathchoice{{\frac{d}{dt}\pi\circ c_{\alpha}\,\smash{\vrule height=5.55557pt,depth=1.27501pt}}_{\,t}}{{\frac{d}{dt}\pi\circ c_{\alpha}\,\smash{\vrule height=3.88889pt,depth=1.27501pt}}_{\,t}}{{\frac{d}{dt}\pi\circ c_{\alpha}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.77777pt,depth=0.85pt}}_{\,t}}{{\frac{d}{dt}\pi\circ c_{\alpha}\,\smash{\vrule height=2.77777pt,depth=1.15019pt}}_{\,t}}
in
U_{v}M
.
Conditions
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
are the reasons why the proof of the quantum Van Kampen theorem is slightly more involved than the proof of its classical version in
[
Hat00
]
.

An isotopy between
\alpha
and
\beta
is a smooth map
H:[0,1]^{2}\to UM
such that
H(0,\cdot)=c_{\alpha}(\cdot)
,
H(1,\cdot)=c_{\beta}(\cdot)
and
H(s,\cdot)
satisfies
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
for all
s\in[0,1]
.
Since
\overline{M}_{1}\cup\overline{M}_{2}=M
, we can always find integers
a,b\geq 1
and sequences
0=t_{0}<\ldots<t_{a}=1
and
0=s_{0}<\ldots<s_{b}=1
such that
H([s_{i},s_{i+1}]\times[t_{j},t_{j+1}])
is either included in
U\overline{M}_{1}
or in
U\overline{M}_{2}
for all
0\leq j\leq a-1
,
0\leq i\leq b-1
. The
size
of
H
is the minimal such pair
(a,b)
. We will prove Lemma
6.10
by decreasing induction on
a
and
b
. More precisely, let
\mathcal{P}(a,b)
be the property: if
\alpha,\beta\in P_{1}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2})
are related by an isotopy in
M
of size
(n,m)
with
n\leq a
,
m\leq b
then
\alpha\sim_{\phi}\beta
.

Lemma 6.11
.

If
\mathcal{P}(a,b)
holds, so does
\mathcal{P}(a,b+1)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\alpha,\beta
are related by an isotopy
H
of size
(a,b+1)
with parameters
0=t_{0}<\ldots<t_{a}=1
and
0=s_{0}<\ldots<s_{b}=1
. Let
\eta\in\bm{P}_{1}(\mathbf{M})
be the bottom tangle parametrized by
c_{\eta}(t):=H(s_{a-1},t)
. Let
H_{1}
be the isotopy between
\alpha
and
\eta
defined by
H_{1}(s,t)=H(\frac{s}{s_{a-1}},t)
and
H_{2}
the isotopy between
\eta
and
\beta
be
H_{2}(s,t)=H\left((1-s_{a-1})s+s_{a-1},t\right)
. One can suppose that
\eta
intersects
\Sigma_{0}
transversally in at most
a-1
points. For each such point
v
, choose a path
\gamma_{v}
between
v
and a point in
\mathbb{B}_{M}
not intersecting
\eta
and let
\eta^{\prime}
be the bottom tangle obtained from
\eta
by pushing each
v
along
\gamma_{v}
. So
\eta^{\prime}\subset M_{1}\wedge M_{2}
and we have an isotopy
H_{3}
between
\eta
and
\eta^{\prime}
with support in
\overline{M}_{1}\cap\overline{M}_{2}
. By composing
H_{1}
with
H_{3}
we get an isotopy between
\alpha
and
\eta^{\prime}
of size
(a,b)
so
\alpha\sim_{\phi}\eta^{\prime}
by hypothesis. By composing the inverse of
H_{3}
with
H_{2}
we get an isotopy between
\eta^{\prime}
and
\beta
of size
(a,1)
so
\eta^{\prime}\sim_{\phi}\beta
by hypothesis. Therefore
\alpha\sim_{\phi}\beta
.
∎

Lemma 6.12
.

If
\mathcal{P}(a,1)
holds, so does
\mathcal{P}(a+1,1)
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathcal{P}(a,1)
holds.
Let
\alpha
and
\beta
be related by an isotopy
H
of size
(a+1,1)
and parameters
0=t_{0}<\ldots<t_{a+1}
.
Consider the two squares
R_{1}=[0,t_{1}]\times[0,1]
and
R_{2}=[t_{1},t_{2}]\times[0,1]
. By minimality of
a
,
H(R_{1})
and
H(R_{2})
lies in distinct
\overline{M}_{i}
. Without loss of generality, one can suppose that
H(R_{1})\subset\overline{M}_{1}
and
H(R_{2})\subset\overline{M}_{2}
. Let
e:=\{t_{1}\}\times[0,1]=R_{1}\cap R_{2}
so that
H(e)\subset\overline{M}_{1}\cap\overline{M}_{2}=\mathbb{B}_{M}\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)
. Let
\alpha_{s}
be the bottom tangle parametrized by
H(\cdot,s)
so that
\alpha_{0}=\alpha
and
\alpha_{1}=\beta
.

We want to ”cut” the homotopy
H
in two parts: the restriction to
R_{1}
, seen as an isotopy of size
(1,1)
and the restriction to
[t_{1},1]\times[0,1]
seen an isotopy of size
(a,1)
. The equivalence
\alpha\sim_{\phi}\beta
will then result from
\mathcal{P}(a,1)
. We will proceed in three steps.

Step 1: Reduction to the case where
H(t_{1},1)\in\mathbb{B}_{M}

Let
v_{1}:=\pi(H(t_{1},1))\in\mathbb{B}_{M}\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)
. By slightly isotoping
\beta
, one can suppose that
v_{1}\in\mathbb{B}_{M}\cup\Sigma^{0}
. If
v_{1}\in\Sigma^{0}
, choose
w_{1}\in\mathbb{B}_{M}
and
\gamma
path between
v_{1}
and
w_{1}
not intersecting
\beta
and let
\beta^{\prime}
be the push of
\beta
along
\gamma
. By post-composing
H
with an isotopy between
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
with support in
\mathbb{B}_{M}\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)
, one gets an isotopy
H^{\prime}
between
\alpha
and
\beta^{\prime}
of the same size
(a+1,1)
. Since
\beta\sim_{\phi}\beta^{\prime}
, up to replacing
\beta
and
H
by
\beta^{\prime}
and
H^{\prime}
, we can, and do, suppose that
H(t_{1},1)\in\mathbb{B}_{M}
.

Step 2: Reduction to the case where
H(e)
is a constant vector in
U\mathbb{B}_{M}

For
s\in[0,1]
, write
\overrightarrow{v}_{s}:=H(t_{1},s)\in U(\mathbb{B}_{M}\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I))
and
v_{s}=\pi(\overrightarrow{v}_{s})
. So
s\mapsto v_{s}
parametrizes a smooth path
\delta
in
\mathbb{B}_{M}\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)
between
v_{0}
and
v_{1}\in\mathbb{B}_{M}
. Up to slightly isotoping
\alpha
and
H
, we can suppose that
\delta
does not intersect
\alpha
. Let
\alpha^{\prime}
be the push of
\alpha
along
\delta
and let
H^{0}
be an isotopy between
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\alpha
with support in
\mathbb{B}_{M}\cup\iota_{\Sigma}(\Sigma\times I)
. Let
H^{\prime}
be the isotopy between
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta
obtained by composing
H^{0}
with
H
. By replacing
\alpha
by
\alpha^{\prime}
and
H
by
H^{\prime}
, we are reduced to the case where the path
s\to\overrightarrow{v}_{s}
is a contractible loop in
U\mathbb{B}_{M}
(with base point
\overrightarrow{v}_{1}
). So, up to isotoping
H
, one can (and do) suppose that
H(e)
is the constant vector

\overrightarrow{v}_{1}
.

Step 3: Cutting the isotopy in two

Let
\eta
be the
1
-bottom tangle parametrized by
c_{\eta}(t)=H(1,t)
for
0\leq t\leq t_{1}
and
c_{\eta}(t)=H(0,t)
for
t_{1}\leq t\leq 1
. Define an isotopy
H^{L}
between
\alpha
and
\eta
by
H^{L}(s,t)=H(s,t)
for
0\leq t\leq t_{1}
and
H^{L}(s,t)=H(0,t)
for
t_{1}\leq t\leq 1
. Since
H^{L}
has support in
H(R_{1})\subset\overline{M}_{1}
, one has
\alpha\sim_{\phi}\eta
. Define an isotopy
H^{R}
between
\eta
and
\beta
by
H^{R}(s,t)=H(1,t)
for
0\leq t\leq t_{1}
and
H^{R}(s,t)=H(s,t)
for
t_{1}\leq t\leq 1
. Then
H^{R}
has size
(1,a)
so by hypothesis, we have
\eta\sim_{\phi}\beta
. Therefore
\alpha\sim_{\phi}\beta
.

∎

Proof of Proposition
6.9
.

Since the property
\mathcal{P}(1,1)
is trivially satisfied, by induction on
a
Lemma
6.12
implies that
\mathcal{P}(a,1)
holds for every
a\geq 1
and by induction on
b
, Lemma
6.11
implies that
\mathcal{P}(a,b)
holds for every
a,b\geq 1
. Therefore Proposition
6.9
holds for
n=1
and so, by Lemma
6.10
, it holds for every
n\geq 1
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.5
.

The theorem follows from the commutativity of the square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{P_{\Sigma}}P_{M_{2}}(\mathbf{H}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M}(\mathbf{H}_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$P_{n}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim_{\phi}$}\right.}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$P_{n}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{M}_{2})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim_{\psi}$}\right.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

where the equality on the bottom follows from Proposition
6.9
.

∎

6.2.
Quantum Van Kampen for quantum representation spaces and stated skein modules

Let
\mathbf{M}_{1}
,
\mathbf{M}_{2}
and
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be as in the last subsection. Since
\mathbf{M}_{1}
and
\mathbf{M}_{2}
are right and left modules over
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
in
(\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}},\wedge)
, then
\operatorname{Rep}^{G}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{1})
and
\operatorname{Rep}^{G}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{2})
are left and right modules over
\operatorname{Rep}^{G}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
by monoidality of
\operatorname{Rep}^{G}_{q}
. The following was suggested by Habiro in
[
Hab12
]
.

Theorem 6.13
.

One has an isomorphism
\operatorname{Rep}^{G}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2})\cong\operatorname{Rep}^{G}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{1})\otimes_{\operatorname{Rep}^{G}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}\operatorname{Rep}^{G}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{2})
.

Proof.

By Theorem
6.5
, the following is a coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{D}P_{\Sigma}\otimes_{D}P_{M_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{D}P_{M_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{1}\otimes id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes\nabla_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\pi)}

thus the following sequence is exact:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[P_{M_{1}}]\otimes_{D}k[P_{\Sigma}]\otimes_{D}k[P_{M_{2}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[P_{M_{1}}]\otimes_{D}k[P_{M_{2}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k[P_{M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{1}\otimes id-id\otimes\nabla_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\pi)}

Since tensoring on the right preserves right exact sequences, the following sequence is exact as well:

(k[P_{M_{1}}]\otimes_{D}k[P_{\Sigma}]\otimes_{D}k[P_{M_{2}}])\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}}Q_{B_{q}G}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{1}\otimes id-id\otimes\nabla_{2}}(k[P_{M_{1}}]\otimes_{D}k[P_{M_{2}}])\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}}Q_{B_{q}G}\\
\xrightarrow{P(\pi)}k[P_{M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}}]\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}}Q_{B_{q}G}\to 0.

Using Lemmas
3.1
and
3.10
, we get the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{1})\otimes_{\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{1})\otimes\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\mathbf{M}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{1}\otimes id-id\otimes\nabla_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P(\pi)}

which says that
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\mathbf{M}_{2})\cong\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{1})\otimes_{\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{2})
.
∎

Putting Corollary
4.15
and Theorem
6.5
together, we obtain the following reformulation of Theorem
1.3
.

Corollary 6.14
.

One has an isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}_{2})\cong\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{2})
.

One can derive from Corollary
6.14
and Theorem
4.16
an alternative proof of a theorem of Gunningham-Jordan-Safronov in
[
GJS
]
as follows. Let
M
be a closed compact connected oriented
3
-manifold and suppose that
M=N_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}N_{2}
is obtained by gluing two (compact, oriented, connected)
3
-manifolds along their connected boundary
\Sigma
. Choose a ball
\mathbb{B}^{3}\subset M
which intersects
\Sigma\subset M
along a disc
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{B}^{3}\cap\Sigma
such that
\mathbb{D}
cuts
\mathbb{B}^{3}
in two hemispheres. Let
\mathbb{D}_{M}\subset\partial\mathbb{B}^{3}
be a disc such that
\mathbb{D}_{M}
intersects
\mathbb{D}
along a closed interval
a
which cuts
\mathbb{D}_{M}
into two half-discs
\mathbb{D}_{M}^{+}\subset N_{1}
and
\mathbb{D}_{M}^{-}\subset N_{2}
. Choose an oriented homeomorphism
\iota_{M}:\mathbb{D}^{2}\cong\mathbb{D}_{M}
sending
\mathbb{D}^{\pm}
to
\mathbb{D}_{M}^{\pm}
and set
\iota_{\pm}:\mathbb{D}_{2}\cong\mathbb{D}_{\pm}\xrightarrow{\iota_{M}}\mathbb{D}_{M}^{\pm}
.
Let
\mathbf{M}:=(M\setminus\mathring{\mathbb{B}}^{3},\iota_{M})\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
and consider
\mathbf{N}_{1}:=(N_{1}\setminus\mathring{\mathbb{B}}^{3}_{+},\iota^{+})
and
\mathbf{N}_{2}:=(N_{2}\setminus\mathring{\mathbb{B}}^{3}_{-},\iota^{-})
and write
\mathbf{\Sigma}:=(\Sigma\setminus\mathring{\mathbb{D}},a)\in\operatorname{MS}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
such that
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{N}_{1}\cup_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\mathbf{N}_{2}
. By Theorem
4.16
, the embedding
\iota:M\to\mathbf{M}
induces an isomorphism
\iota_{*}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(M)\cong(\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}))^{coinv}
and by Corollary
6.14
, one has an isomorphism
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M})\cong\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{N}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{N}_{2})
. Therefore, using Theorem
4.26
, we have re-proved the

Theorem 6.15
.

(Gunningham-Jordan-Safronov
[
GJS
, Theorem
2
, Corollary
1
]
) We have isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{q}(M)\cong\left(\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{N}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{N}_{2})\right)^{coinv},\quad\mbox{ and }

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(M)\cong\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(\mathbf{N}_{1})\otimes_{\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(\mathbf{N}_{2}).

Applied to a Heegaard splitting
M=H_{g}\cup_{\Sigma_{g}}H_{g}
of
M
, this theorem, together with an algebraic analogue of a theorem of Kashiwara-Schapira, was proved in
[
GJS
, Theorem
1
]
to imply that
\mathcal{S}^{rat}_{q}(M)
has finite dimension. Note that, because of Corollary
5.9
,
\mathcal{S}_{q}(M)
is infinitely generated whenever the character variety of
M
is not
0
dimensional so the torsion part of
\mathcal{S}_{q}(M)
is infinitely generated in this case.

6.3.
Self-gluing and quantum HNN extensions

In the last subsections, we have considered the operation of gluing two
3
manifolds along a surface and proved that
P_{M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}M_{2}}
is isomorphic to
P_{M_{1}}\otimes_{P_{\Sigma}}P_{M_{2}}
. We now consider the operation, giving a
3
manifold
M
and two attaching maps
\phi_{1}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow\partial M
,
\phi_{2}:\overline{\Sigma}\hookrightarrow\partial M
of gluing the two copies of
\Sigma
together to get a new manifold
M_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}
and will prove that
P_{M_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{HH}^{0}(P_{\Sigma},P_{M})
, where
\mathrm{HH}^{0}
is defined in a braided sense. We will proceed by identifying
M_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}
with
\Sigma\times I\cup_{\overline{\Sigma}\wedge\Sigma}M
and use the quantum Van Kampen theorem. The proof will then be a braided version of the classical proof of the fact that for
M
a bimodule over some algebra
A
, then
\mathrm{HH}^{0}(A,M)
is isomorphic to
A\otimes_{A^{op}\otimes A}M
.

Let
\mathbf{M}\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
,
\mathbf{\Sigma}\in\operatorname{MS}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
and
\phi_{1}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow\partial M
,
\phi_{2}:\overline{\Sigma}\hookrightarrow\partial M
two embeddings such that:
(1)
the boundary arc
a
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is sent to
\partial_{+}\mathbb{D}_{M}
by both
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
and
(2)
the
images of
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
intersect in a contractible neighborhood of
\partial_{+}\mathbb{D}_{M}
. We define an embedding
\phi:\overline{\Sigma}\wedge\Sigma\hookrightarrow\partial M
, illustrated in Figure
20
, as follows.

Divide the arc
\partial_{+}\mathbb{D}_{M}
into two segments
\partial_{+}\mathbb{D}_{M}=\partial_{+,L}\mathbb{D}_{M}\cup\partial_{+,R}\mathbb{D}_{M}
where
\partial_{+,L}\mathbb{D}_{M}=\iota_{M}\left(\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}\cap\mathbb{R}^{\leq 0}\times\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}\right)
and
\partial_{+,R}\mathbb{D}_{M}=\iota_{M}\left(\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}\cap\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{R}^{\geq 0}\right)
. Write
\{p^{+}\}=\partial_{+,L}\mathbb{D}_{M}\cap\partial_{+,R}\mathbb{D}_{M}
. Isotope
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
slightly in the neighborhood of
\partial\mathbb{D}_{M}
such that
(1)

\phi_{1}
maps
a
to
\partial_{+,L}\mathbb{D}_{M}
and
\phi_{2}
maps
a
to
\partial_{+,R}\mathbb{D}_{M}
and
(2)
the intersection of the images of
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
is
\{p^{+}\}
. Define an embedding
\phi:\overline{\Sigma}\wedge\Sigma\hookrightarrow\partial M
by pushing slightly
\partial_{+,L}\mathbb{D}_{M}
and
\partial_{+,R}\mathbb{D}_{M}
inside
\partial M
and considering an embedding of the triangle
T
with edges
\partial_{+}\mathbb{D}_{M}
,
\partial_{+,L}\mathbb{D}_{M}
and
\partial_{+,R}\mathbb{D}_{M}
. This embedding together with
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
glue together to define
\phi
.

Consider the cylinder
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
and define an embedding
\psi:\Sigma\wedge\overline{\Sigma}\hookrightarrow\partial(\Sigma\times I)
as follows. Write
\partial_{-}\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}=\partial_{-,L}\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\cup\partial_{-,R}\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
as before. Isotope the embedding
\Sigma\cong\Sigma\times\{+1\}\hookrightarrow\partial(\Sigma\times I)
to get an embedding
\psi_{1}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow\partial(\Sigma\times I)
sending
a
to
\partial_{-,R}\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
. Isotope the embedding
\overline{\Sigma}\cong\Sigma\times\{-1\}\hookrightarrow\partial\Sigma\times I
to get an embedding
\psi_{2}:\overline{\Sigma}\hookrightarrow\partial(\Sigma\times I)
sending
a
to
\partial_{-,L}\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
. Like before, push slightly
\partial_{-,L}\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
and
\partial_{+,L}\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
inside and define an embedding of the triangle with edges
\partial_{-}\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
,
\partial_{-,L}\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
and
\partial_{-,R}\mathbb{D}_{\mathbf{\Sigma}}
. This embedding together with
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
define the embedding
\psi:\Sigma\wedge\overline{\Sigma}\hookrightarrow\partial(\Sigma\times I)
.

Figure 20.
An illustration of the self-gluing operation where
\mathbf{M}=\mathbb{D}_{2}\times I
, the attaching map
\phi_{1}
is given by a braid and
\phi_{2}
is given by the identity. The resulting manifold
\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}
is the exterior of the link obtained by closing the braid (here the Hopf link) with a ball removed.

Definition 6.16
.

The marked
3
manifold
\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}\in\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
is the gluing
\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}:=(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\cup_{\overline{\Sigma}\wedge\Sigma}\mathbf{M}
defined by the two attaching maps
\phi
and
\psi
. We denote by
\Psi:\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}
the natural embedding.

While gluing the cylinder
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
to
\mathbf{M}
using the gluing maps
\phi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
we get a marked
3
-manifold
(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}
isomorphic to
\mathbf{M}
, so the gluing map defines an embedding

\nabla_{\phi_{1}}^{L}:(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\wedge\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{M}
. Similarly, while gluing
\mathbf{M}
with the cylinder
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
using
\phi_{2}
and
\psi_{1}
, we also get a marked
3
-manifold
(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\cup_{\Sigma}\mathbf{M}
isomorphic to
\mathbf{M}
, so the gluing map defines an embedding
\nabla_{\phi_{2}}^{R}:\mathbf{M}\wedge(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\to\mathbf{M}
.

Definition 6.17
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,c_{\cdot,\cdot},\theta_{\cdot})
be a braided balanced category,
A\in\mathcal{C}
an algebra object and
M\in\mathcal{C}
an
A
-bimodule object with left and right modules maps
\nabla^{L}:A\otimes M\to M
and
\nabla^{R}:M\otimes A\to M
. The
0
-th Hochschild cohomology group
\mathrm{HH}^{0}(A,M)
is (when it exists) the coequalizer

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{HH}^{0}(A,M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{R}\circ c_{A,M}\circ(\theta_{A}\otimes id_{M})}

Remark 6.18
.

Note that if
(M,\nabla^{R})
is a right
A
-module object in
\mathcal{C}
then
(M,\nabla^{R}\circ c_{A,M}\circ(\theta_{A}\otimes id_{M}))
is a left
A
-module object.

The maps
\nabla_{\phi_{1}}^{L}
and
\nabla_{\phi_{2}}^{R}
give
\mathbf{M}
the structure of a bimodule over
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
in
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
. Composing with
P:\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}\to\widehat{\operatorname{BT}}
, we obtain that
P_{M}
is a
P_{\Sigma}
bimodule in
\widehat{\operatorname{BT}}
. Similarly composing with
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}
, we obtain that
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M})
is a bimodule over
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}}
.

Theorem 6.19
.

(1)

One has
P_{M_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}}=\mathrm{HH}^{0}(P_{\Sigma},P_{M})
.

(2)

One has
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}})=\mathrm{HH}^{0}(\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}))
and
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}})=\mathrm{HH}^{0}(\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{M}))
.

By the quantum Van Kampen Theorem
6.5
, the image by
P:\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}\to\widehat{\operatorname{BT}}
of the following sequence is a coequalizer (here and henceforth we write
\mathbf{\Sigma}
instead of
\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I
for simplicity):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge(\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma})\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{1}\wedge id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\wedge\nabla_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\prime}}

We want to deduce from this fact that the image by
P
of the following sequence is a coequalizer too:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{L}_{\phi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{\phi_{2}}^{R}\circ\Psi_{\Sigma,M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

To analyze the morphisms
\nabla_{1}
and
\nabla_{2}
, let us introduce the homeomorphism
\operatorname{ht}_{\Sigma}:\overline{\Sigma}\times I\xrightarrow{\cong}\Sigma\times I
which consists in performing a half-twist in a tubular neighborhood of the boundary disc
\mathbb{D}_{\Sigma}
while leaving the rest invariant. The morphism
\operatorname{ht}_{\Sigma}
does not define a morphism
\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\to\mathbf{\Sigma}
in
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
because it reverses the height order of the boundary disc instead of preserving it. However, for any other marked
3
-manifold
\mathbf{N}
one can define an isomorphism
id\wedge\operatorname{ht}_{\Sigma}:\mathbf{N}\wedge\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbf{N}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma}
in
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{c}}^{(1)}
which consists in performing a half-twist in a neighborhood of
\mathbb{D}_{\overline{\Sigma}}
. Then by definition of the gluing maps, the morphisms
\nabla_{1}
,
\nabla_{2}
decompose as

\nabla_{1}:\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{id\wedge\operatorname{ht}_{\Sigma}^{-1}\wedge id}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\psi_{\Sigma,\Sigma}\wedge id}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\mu^{(2)}_{\Sigma}}\mathbf{\Sigma}

and

\nabla_{2}:\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}\xrightarrow{id\wedge\nabla_{\phi_{1}}^{L}}\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{M}\xrightarrow{\psi_{\overline{\Sigma},M}}\mathbf{M}\wedge\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\xrightarrow{id\wedge\operatorname{ht}_{\Sigma}}\mathbf{M}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\phi_{2}}^{R}}\mathbf{M}.

Define a morphism
j_{1}:\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge(\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma})\wedge\mathbf{M}\to\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}
as the composition:

j_{1}:\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge(\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma})\wedge\mathbf{M}\xrightarrow{id\wedge\operatorname{ht}_{\Sigma}^{-1}\wedge id\wedge id}\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}\\
\xrightarrow{\psi_{\Sigma,\Sigma}\wedge id\wedge id}\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}\xrightarrow{id\wedge\mu_{\Sigma}\wedge id}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}\xrightarrow{id\wedge\nabla_{\phi_{1}}^{L}}\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M},

and set
j_{2}:=\nabla_{\phi_{1}}^{L}
. Then in the diagram

(8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge(\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma})\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{1}\wedge id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\wedge\nabla_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{L}_{\phi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{\phi_{2}}^{R}\circ\Psi_{\Sigma,M}\circ(\theta_{\Sigma}\wedge id_{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}

the three following subdiagrams commute (up to isotopy):

(9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge(\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma})\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{1}\wedge id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{L}_{\phi_{1}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge(\overline{\mathbf{\Sigma}}\wedge\mathbf{\Sigma})\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\wedge\nabla_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla_{\phi_{2}}^{R}\circ\Psi_{\Sigma,M}\circ(\theta_{\Sigma}\wedge id_{M})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
