Proof.

First, the equality
h^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}=h^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime}
follows from the fact that
j_{1}
is an epimorphism together with the commutativity of the diagrams in
(i)
and
(iii)
.
Let
k:B^{\prime}\to E
be a morphism such that
k\circ f^{\prime}=k\circ g^{\prime}
and let us prove that there exists a unique morphism
m:D\to E
such that
k=m\circ h^{\prime}
. Since
k\circ f^{\prime}\circ j_{1}=k\circ g^{\prime}\circ j_{1}
by commutativity of the first two diagrams in Hypothesis
(i)
, one has
(k\circ j_{2})\circ f=(k\circ j_{2})\circ g
. By
(iii)
, there exists a unique
m:D\to E
such that
k\circ j_{2}=m\circ h
. By commutativity of the third diagram in
(i)
, the last identity is equivalent to
k\circ j_{2}=m\circ h^{\prime}\circ j_{2}
and since
j_{2}
is an epimorphism
(ii)
, this is equivalent to
k=m\circ h^{\prime}
. This proves the claim.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.19
.

We apply Lemma
6.20
to the image by
P
of Diagram
8
. The commutativity of Diagrams
9
implies hypothesis
(i)
. Hypothesis
(ii)
follows from the fact that, for
k=1,2
, the codomain of
j_{k}
retracts by deformation on its image, therefore for every
n\geq 0
,
P_{n}(j_{k})
is surjective, so
P(j_{k})
is an epimorphism. Hypothesis
(iii)
follows from the quantum Van Kampen Theorem
6.5
. Therefore
P_{M_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}}=\mathrm{HH}^{0}(P_{\Sigma},P_{M})
. The corresponding results for
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}
and
\mathcal{S}_{q}
are deduced from the fact that
\bullet\otimes_{\operatorname{BT}}Q_{B_{q}G}
preserves right exact sequences exactly in the same manner than in the proof of Theorem
6.13
so we leave the details to the reader.
∎

7.
Quantum representation spaces of mapping tori

7.1.
The quantum adjoint coaction

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma,\{a\})\in\operatorname{MS}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}
be a connected
1
-marked surface let
\operatorname{ad}_{\Sigma}:\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I\to(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\wedge\mathbf{H}_{1}
be the embedding whose image by the quantum fundamental group functor is the morphism
P(\operatorname{ad}_{\Sigma})
illustrated in Figure
21
. More precisely, consider the marked surface
\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge{\mathbb{D}_{1}}
obtained from
\mathbf{\Sigma}\bigsqcup\mathbb{D}_{1}\bigsqcup(\mathbb{D}^{2},\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\})
by gluing
e_{1}
with
a
and
e_{2}
with the boundary arc of the annulus
\mathbb{D}_{1}
. In particular,
(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\wedge\mathbf{H}_{1}
is the image by the functor
\cdot\times I:\operatorname{MS}\to\mathcal{M}
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{\mathbb{D}_{1}}
.
Define an embedding
\operatorname{ad}^{0}:\Sigma\to\Sigma\wedge\mathbb{D}_{1}
whose restriction outside a collar neighborhood
N(a)\cong[0,1]^{2}
of
a
is the identity and sending
N(a)
to a band between
a
and
e_{3}
making one turn around the hole of the annulus
\mathbb{D}_{1}
. Then
\operatorname{ad}_{\Sigma}
is the image of
\operatorname{ad}^{0}
by
\cdot\times I:\operatorname{MS}\to\mathcal{M}
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{\mathbb{D}_{1}}
.

Figure 21.
An illustration of the quantum adjoint coaction.

Definition 7.1
.

The
quantum adjoint coactions
are the morphisms
\operatorname{Ad}_{\Sigma}:\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\overline{\otimes}B_{q}G
and
\operatorname{Ad}_{\Sigma}:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\to\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\overline{\otimes}B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
obtained by taking the images of
\operatorname{ad}_{\Sigma}
by
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}
and
\mathcal{S}_{q}
respectively and using the lax monoidality.

The quantum adjoint coaction equips
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
(resp.
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
) with a structure of
B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
(resp.
B_{q}G
) right comodule object in the braided category
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}}
).
In the particular case where
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\mathbf{H}_{1}
, we have proved in Theorem
4.10
that
\operatorname{Ad}_{\mathbf{H}_{1}}
coincides with Majid’s braided adjoint coaction. In particular,
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
is an
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
right comodule in
\operatorname{Mod}_{k}
and a
B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
right comodule in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
. Recall from Section
4.2
the isomorphism
f:B\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
where
B\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
is equal to
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})
as a co-algebra.

Lemma 7.2
.

The following diagram (in
\operatorname{Mod}_{k}
) commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\otimes\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbb{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id\otimes f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{R}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ad}_{\Sigma}}

In particular, the subspace of
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
coinvariant vectors of
\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
coincides with its subspace of
B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
coinvariant vectors.

Proof.

Let
[T,s]=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{QAdPf1.eps}}}}}}}$}\in\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})
be the class of an arbitrary stated tangle in
\mathbf{\Sigma}
. Applying the skein relation in Remark
4.2
, one finds:

\mathcal{S}_{q}(\operatorname{ad}_{\Sigma})([T,s])=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{QAdPf2.eps}}}}}}}$}=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{QAdPf3.eps}}}}}}}$}\xrightarrow[\cong]{\Psi}\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{QAdPf4.eps}}}}}}}$},

where
\Psi:\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma}\wedge\mathbf{H}_{1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\overline{\otimes}\mathcal{S}_{q}(\mathbf{H}_{1})
is the isomorphism of Section
4.1.3
.
Applying
id\otimes f^{-1}
, we obtain:

(id\otimes f^{-1})\operatorname{Ad}_{\Sigma}([T,s])=\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{QAdPf5.eps}}}}}}}$}=\Delta^{R}_{a}([T,s]).

∎

7.2.
Mapping tori

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}=(\Sigma,\{a\})\in\operatorname{MS}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}
be a connected
1
-marked surface and denote by
\partial
the connected component of
\partial\Sigma
containing
a
. Let
\phi:\Sigma\to\Sigma
be an oriented diffeomorphism whose restriction to
\partial
is the identity. Let
M_{\phi}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\Sigma\times I$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\left((\phi(x),+1)\sim(x,-1),x\in\Sigma\right)$}\right.}
be the associated mapping torus and
\pi:\Sigma\times I\to M_{\phi}
the quotient map. Consider a parametrized disc
\mathbb{D}_{M_{\phi}}\subset\partial M_{\phi}
contained in the image of
\partial\times I
and let
\mathbf{M}_{\phi}:=(M_{\phi},\{\mathbb{D}_{M_{\phi}}\})\in\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}
. The goal of this section is to describe
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{\phi})
.

Let
\mathbf{M}:=(\mathbf{\Sigma}\times I)\wedge\mathbf{H}_{1}=(\Sigma\wedge\mathbb{D}_{1})\times I
and consider two attaching maps
\phi_{1}:\Sigma\hookrightarrow\partial M
and
\phi_{2}:\overline{\Sigma}\hookrightarrow\partial M
defined as follows.

•

The embedding
\phi_{2}:\overline{\Sigma}\to\partial M_{1}
is the composition
\phi_{2}:\overline{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\overline{\operatorname{ad}_{\Sigma}^{0}}}\overline{\Sigma\wedge\mathbb{D}_{1}}\cong(\Sigma\wedge\mathbb{D}_{1})\times\{-1\}\subset\partial M
.

•

The embedding
\phi_{1}:\Sigma\to\partial M_{1}
is the composition
\phi_{1}:\Sigma\xrightarrow{\phi}\Sigma\xrightarrow{\iota_{1}}\Sigma\wedge\mathbb{D}_{1}\cong(\Sigma\wedge\mathbb{D}_{1})\times\{+1\}\subset\partial M
.

Then
\mathbf{M}_{\phi_{1}\#\phi_{2}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{M}_{\phi}
, so Theorem
6.19
implies that
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{\phi})\cong\mathrm{HH}^{0}(\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma}),\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}))
. Note that, since
\mathbf{M}
is a thickened surface, it has an algebra structure with product
\mu_{M}
. Write
\mu_{M}^{top}:=\mu_{M}\circ\psi_{M,M}\circ(\theta_{M}\wedge id_{M}):M\wedge M\to M
the
twisted opposite product
. By Remark
6.18
,
\mu_{M}^{top}
is not really a product since it is not associative but rather equips
M
with a structure of left module over itself in the sense that one has the equality

\mu^{top}_{M}(\mu_{M}\otimes id_{M})=\mu^{top}_{M}(id_{M}\otimes\mu^{top}_{M}).

By definition, the module morphisms
\nabla_{\phi_{1}}^{L}
and
\nabla_{\phi_{2}}^{R}
decompose as
\nabla_{\phi_{1}}^{L}=\mu_{M}^{top}\circ(\operatorname{ad}_{\Sigma}\wedge id)
and
\nabla_{\phi_{2}}^{R}=\mu_{M}\circ(\iota_{1}\phi\wedge id)
. Therefore we have proved the

Theorem 7.3
.

The following sequence is exact:

\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\overline{\otimes}(\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\overline{\otimes}B_{q}G)\xrightarrow{\mu\circ(\iota_{1}\phi_{*}\otimes id)-\mu^{top}\circ(\operatorname{Ad}_{\Sigma}\otimes id)}\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{\Sigma})\overline{\otimes}B_{q}G\to\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{\phi})\to 0.

See Figure
22
for an illustration.

Figure 22.
An illustration of the equality

P\left(\mu^{top}\circ(\operatorname{ad}_{\Sigma}\wedge id)\right)(\alpha\otimes\gamma)=P\left(\mu\circ(\iota_{1}\beta_{*}\otimes id)\right)(\alpha\otimes\gamma)

in
P_{M_{\beta}}
where
\Sigma=\mathbb{D}_{2}
,
\beta
a
2
strands braid,
\alpha\in P_{1}(\mathbb{D}_{2})
and
\beta\in P_{1}(\mathbb{D}_{2}\wedge\mathbf{H}_{1})
.

8.
Quantum representation spaces of links exterior

8.1.
Links exterior

Let
\beta\in B_{n}
be a braid, seen as a mapping class of
\mathbb{D}_{n}
, and let
L\subset S^{3}
be the link obtained by closing
\beta
. Since
\mathbf{H}_{n}=\mathbb{D}_{n}\times I
, the mapping class
\beta
induces a morphism (still denoted by the same letter)
\beta:\mathbf{H}_{n}\to\mathbf{H}_{n}
in
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}
.
Let
\mathbf{M}_{L}\in\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\operatorname{c}}
be the marked
3
manifold where
M_{L}=S^{3}\setminus(\mathring{N}(L)\cup\mathring{B}^{3})
is obtained by removing from
S^{3}
the union of the interior of a tubular neighborhood of
L
and an open ball, and with a single boundary disc
\mathbb{D}_{L}\subset\partial\mathbb{B}^{3}
. The product
\mu_{\mathbb{D}_{n}}:\mathbb{D}_{n}\wedge\mathbb{D}_{n}\to\mathbb{D}_{n}
defines a product
\mu:\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbb{D}_{n})^{\overline{\otimes}2}\to\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbb{D}_{n})
. Set
\mu^{top}:=\mu\circ\psi\circ(\theta\otimes id)
.

Theorem 8.1
.

One has an exact sequence in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{G}}
:

\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbb{D}_{n})\overline{\otimes}\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbb{D}_{n})\xrightarrow{\mu^{top}-\mu\circ(\beta_{*}\otimes id)}\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbb{D}_{n})\to\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{L})\to 0.

Said differently, identifying
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbb{D}_{n})
with
(B_{q}G)^{\overline{\otimes}n}
, one has

\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{G}(\mathbf{M}_{L})\cong{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$(B_{q}G)^{\overline{\otimes}n}$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\left(\mu^{top}(x\otimes y)-\beta_{*}(x)y\right)$}\right.}.

Proof.

The marked
3
-manifold
\mathbf{M}_{L}
is obtained from
\mathbf{M}:=\mathbb{D}_{n}\times I
by self-gluing using the attaching maps
\phi_{1}:\mathbb{D}_{n}\hookrightarrow\partial M
,
\phi_{1}:\mathbb{D}_{n}\xrightarrow{\beta}\mathbb{D}_{n}\cong\mathbb{D}_{n}\times\{+1\}\subset\partial(\mathbb{D}_{n}\times I)
and
\phi_{2}:\overline{\mathbb{D}_{n}}\hookrightarrow\partial M
,
\phi_{2}:\overline{\mathbb{D}_{n}}\cong\mathbb{D}_{n}\times\{-1\}\subset\partial(\mathbb{D}_{n}\times I)
. The left module map
\nabla_{\phi_{1}}^{L}
is the composition
\mu\circ(\beta_{*}\otimes id)
and the right module map
\nabla_{\phi_{2}}^{R}
is just the product
\mu_{\mathbb{D}_{n}}
so the results follows from Theorem
6.19
.
∎

Corollary 8.2
.

Let
M_{L}=S^{3}\setminus N(L)\in\mathcal{M}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{S}_{q}^{rat}(M_{L})
be the standard (non stated) skein module. Then

\mathcal{S}_{q}^{rat}(M_{L})\cong{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{rat})^{\overline{\otimes}n}$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\left(\mu^{top}(x\otimes y)-\beta_{*}(x)y\right)$}\right.}.

8.2.
Comparison with the constructions in
[
MVdV
]

We preserve the notations of the last subsection. The following was introduced by the second author together with Van-der-Veen in
[
MVdV
]
:

Definition 8.3
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}\subset(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\overline{\otimes}n}
be the right ideal generated by elements
\beta_{*}(x)-x
. We set

\mathcal{A}_{\beta}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\overline{\otimes}n}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{I}_{\beta}$}\right.}.

It is proved in
[
MVdV
]
that
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
is a bilateral ideal preserved by the
\mathcal{O}_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
coaction so
\mathcal{A}_{\beta}
is an algebra in
\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}}
. Moreover the adjoint coaction
\operatorname{Ad}:(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\overline{\otimes}n}\to(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\overline{\otimes}n}\overline{\otimes}B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
sends
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}
inside
\mathcal{I}_{\beta}\otimes B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
so induces a comodule morphism (still denoted by the same letter)
\operatorname{Ad}:\mathcal{A}_{\beta}\to\mathcal{A}_{\beta}\otimes B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
which is an algebra morphism.

Definition 8.4
.

The subalgebra
\mathcal{A}_{\beta}^{coinv}\subset\mathcal{A}_{\beta}
is the subalgebra of coinvariant vectors for the
\operatorname{Ad}
coaction.

The main result of
[
MVdV
]
is the fact that if
\beta
and
\beta^{\prime}
are two braids which admit the same Markov closure, then
\mathcal{A}_{\beta}\cong\mathcal{A}_{\beta^{\prime}}
in
\operatorname{Alg}(\overline{\mathcal{C}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}})
therefore the isomorphism classes of both
\mathcal{A}_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\beta}^{coinv}
only depends on the link
L
. In
[
MVdV
]
, the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\beta}
was called
quantum representation variety
whereas
\mathcal{A}_{\beta}^{coinv}
was named
quantum character variety
of
L
. The initial motivation for the present paper was to relate these algebras to Habiro’s quantum representation and character varieties and to (stated) skein modules. As can be shown by taking a simple example (such as the trivial knot) the two modules
\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{M}_{L})
and
\mathcal{A}_{\beta}
are not isomorphic. However Theorem
8.1
shows that they are very similar; indeed one has

\mathcal{A}_{\beta}={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\overline{\otimes}n}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\left(\mu(x\otimes y)-\beta_{*}(x)y\right)$}\right.}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\operatorname{Rep}_{q}^{\operatorname{SL}_{2}}(\mathbf{M}_{L})\cong{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$(B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2})^{\overline{\otimes}n}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\left(\mu^{top}(x\otimes y)-\beta_{*}(x)y\right)$}\right.}

so we just have changed
\mu
to
\mu^{top}
in the quotient. Moreover, since taking the coinvariant vectors for the
\mathcal{O}_{q}[\operatorname{SL}_{2}]
coaction than for the braided
B_{q}\operatorname{SL}_{2}
quantum coaction by Lemma
7.2
, the skein module of
M_{L}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\beta}^{coinv}
are very similar. We expect that the computational techniques developed in
[
Mur21
]
can be adapted to compute the peripheral ideal of links exteriors using this analogy.

Appendix A
Finite presentation of the category of bottom tangles

In this appendix, we explain how the works of Kerler
[
Ker99
,
Ker03
]
and Bobtcheva-Piergallini
[
BP12
]
permit to prove the following reformulation of Theorem
2.15
:

Theorem A.1
.

The category
\operatorname{BT}
is presented by the generators
(\mu,\eta,\Delta,\epsilon,S^{\pm 1},\theta)
of Figure
5
and the relations of Figure
4
.

Let us fix the terminology used here. Let
\mathcal{C}
be a PROP, i.e. a monoidal category with set of objects
\mathbb{N}
and such that
a\otimes b=a+b
, and consider a set
G
of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
. For a morphism
\mu
of
\mathcal{C}
, we write
s(\mu),t(\mu)\in\mathbb{N}
the integers (source and target) such that
\mu:s(\mu)\to t(\mu)
. The set
G
is said to
generate

\mathcal{C}
if for every
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
, every morphism in
\mathcal{C}(a,b)
can be expressed as a composition of elements of the form
\mathds{1}_{x}\otimes g\otimes\mathds{1}_{y}
with
g\in G
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{G}
be the PROP freely generated by
G
. By definition, the morphisms in
\mathcal{F}_{G}(a,b)
are formal expressions of the form
\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}
where
\mu_{i}=\mathds{1}_{x_{i}}\otimes g_{i}\otimes\mathds{1}_{y_{i}}
with
g_{i}\in G
, such that
s(\mu_{i})=t(\mu_{i+1})
,
s(\mu_{1})=a
,
t(\mu_{n})=b
. Composition is the concatenation of words. Equivalently, we can represent the morphisms in
\mathcal{F}_{G}(a,b)
by graphs with
a
leaves on the top and
b
leaves on the bottom and possibly some
(s(g),t(g))
coupons for each
g\in G
. For instance, letting
G
be the set of morphisms of
\operatorname{BT}
of Figure
5
, the morphism
\mu(\mathds{1}\otimes S)\Delta\in\mathcal{F}_{G}(1,1)
is represented by the graph

. There is a unique monoidal functor
F:\mathcal{F}_{G}\to\mathcal{C}
sending
g\in\mathcal{F}_{G}(s(g),t(g))
to
g\in\mathcal{C}(s(g),t(g))
and
G
is a generating set if and only if
F
is essentially surjective.

An
equivalence relation
on a PROP
\mathcal{F}
is the data for each
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
of an equivalence relation
\sim
on the set
\mathcal{F}(a,b)
in such a way that
\mu\sim\nu
implies that
\eta\otimes\mu\otimes\kappa\sim\eta\otimes\nu\otimes\kappa
and that
\mu\sim\nu
implies that
\mu^{\prime}\circ\mu\circ\mu^{\prime\prime}\sim\mu^{\prime}\circ\nu\circ\mu^{\prime\prime}
(i.e.
\sim
is preserved by composition and tensor product). In this case we can define a new PROP
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{F}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim$}\right.}
by
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{F}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim$}\right.}(a,b):={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{F}(a,b)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim$}\right.}
. For instance, if
G
is a set of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
and
F:\mathcal{F}_{G}\to\mathcal{C}
the associated functor, we define an equivalence
\sim_{\mathcal{C}}
in
\mathcal{F}_{G}
by setting
\mu\sim_{\mathcal{C}}\nu
if
F(\mu)=F(\nu)
. When
G
generates
\mathcal{C}
, then
F:{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{F}_{G}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\sim_{\mathcal{C}}$}\right.}\to\mathcal{C}
is an equivalence of categories. A
relation
in
\mathcal{C}
is a pair
(\mu,\nu)
of morphisms in
\mathcal{F}_{G}
with the same target and source and such that
F(\mu)=F(\nu)
. For instance
\left(\mu(\mu\otimes\mathds{1}),\mu(\mathds{1}\otimes\mu)\right)
is the relation in
\operatorname{BT}
corresponding to the fact that
\mu
is associative. Define a poset structure
\leq
on the set
ER
of equivalence relations on
\mathcal{F}_{G}
by setting
\sim_{1}\leq\sim_{2}
if
\mu\sim_{1}\nu
implies
\mu\sim_{2}\nu
. The poset
(ER,\leq)
is clearly filtrant: given
\sim_{1}
and
\sim_{2}
the relation
\sim
defined by
\mu\sim\nu
if we have both
\mu\sim_{1}\nu
and
\mu\sim_{2}\nu
clearly satisfies
\sim\leq\sim_{1}
and
\sim\leq\sim_{2}
. Therefore, by Zorn lemma, given a set
Rel
of relations, we can speak of the smallest equivalence relation
\sim_{Rel}
containing
Rel
; we say that
Rel
is a
complete set of relations
if
\sim_{Rel}=\sim_{\mathcal{C}}
.

Definition A.2
.

For
\mathcal{C}
a PROP,
G
a set of morphisms and
Rel
a set of relations, we say that
(G,Rel)
is a
presentation
of
\mathcal{C}
if
G
generates
\mathcal{C}
and if
Rel
is a complete set of relations.

Theorem A.3
.

(Habiro
[
Hab06
, Theorem
5.16
]
) The set
G:=\{\mu,\eta,\Delta,\epsilon,S^{\pm 1},\theta\}
of morphisms of Figure
5
generates
\operatorname{BT}
.

Recall from Remark
2.14
the faithful braided functor

\partial:\operatorname{BT}\to\mathcal{C}^{CYK}

sending
\mathbf{H}_{g}
to
\Sigma_{g,1}
and sending an embedding
\mu:\mathbf{H}_{a}\to\mathbf{H}_{b}
to the cobordism
\mathbf{H}_{b}\setminus\mu(\mathbf{H}_{a})
. Not every cobordism in
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}(a,b)
is homeomorphic to a cobordism of the form
\mathbf{H}_{b}\setminus\mu(\mathbf{H}_{a})
(so
\partial
is not full), however every element of
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}(a,b)
can be obtained from a cobordism
\mathbf{H}_{b}\setminus\mu(\mathbf{H}_{a})
by performing some surgery along a closed framed link
L\subset\mathbf{H}_{b}\setminus\mu(\mathbf{H}_{a})
. We can thus represent pictorially a morphism in
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}(a,b)
by a
a
-bottom tangle
T
in
\mathbf{H}_{b}
entangled with a closed framed link
L\subset H_{b}\setminus T
and think of
T\cup L
as the cobordism obtained from
\mathbf{H}_{b}\setminus\mu_{T}(\mathbf{H}_{a})
by performing a surgery along
L
. The pairs
(T,L)
are thus only considered up to some Kirby moves (see
[
Ker99
,
Ker03
,
BP12
]
for a precise definition of the Kirby moves involved). For instance, one can consider the two morphisms:

\lambda:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{BT_integral.eps}}}}}}}$}\in\mathcal{C}^{CYK}(1,0)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\Lambda:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{BT_cointegral.eps}}}}}}}$}\in\mathcal{C}^{CYK}(0,1),

which do not belong to the image of
\partial
(we perform the surgery along the green curve here). They satisfy some relations drawn in Figure
23
which justify the terminology
integral
and
cointegral
for
\lambda
and
\Lambda
respectively.

Figure 23.
The relations satisfied by the integral and cointegral in
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}
.

By abuse of notations, we identify
\operatorname{BT}
with its image by
\partial
inside
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}
so we consider the set
G
of morphisms of Figure
5
both as a set of morphisms in
\operatorname{BT}
and
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}
. Similarly, the set
Rel
of relations defined by Figure
4
is seen as a set of relations for
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}
as well.

Theorem A.4
.

(Bobtcheva-Piergallini
[
BP12
, Theorem
5.5.4
]
) The category
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}
is presented by the set of generators
G\bigsqcup\{\lambda,\Lambda\}
and the set of relations which is the union of
Rel
with the relations of Figure
23
.

That the set
G\bigsqcup\{\lambda,\Lambda\}
generates
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}
and that they satisfy the above relations (with the exception of the two bottom ”BP” relations in Figure
4
) was proved by Kerler in
[
Ker99
]
who conjectured that they form a presentation of
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}
. The two additional BP relations were found by Bobtcheva-Piergallini in
[
BP12
]
.

Proof of Theorem
A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be the category presented by
(G,Rel)
and
f:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}^{CYK}
be the inclusion functor. It suffices to prove that
f
is faithful; indeed, since
\partial:\operatorname{BT}\to\mathcal{C}^{CYK}
is faithful and since
\operatorname{BT}
is generated by
G
by Theorem
A.3
, this will prove that
\operatorname{BT}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{D}
and conclude the proof. Write
G^{\prime}:=G\bigsqcup\{\lambda,\Lambda\}
, let
Rel^{\prime\prime}
be the set of relations (in
\mathcal{F}_{G^{\prime}}
) of Figure
23
and
Rel^{\prime}:=Rel\bigsqcup Rel^{\prime\prime}
so that
\mathcal{C}^{CYK}
is presented by
(G^{\prime},Rel^{\prime})
(in virtue of Theorem
A.4
).

The faithfulness of
f
follows from the following remark: for each relation in
Rel^{\prime\prime}
of Figure
23
, if we replace
\lambda
by the counit
\epsilon
and
\Lambda
by the unit
\eta
, then get a relation in
Rel
. Therefore the quotient category
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{C}^{CYK}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{Integral_quotient.eps}}}}}}}$}\ \right)$}\right.}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{D}
and the quotient functor

g:\mathcal{C}^{CYK}\to{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{C}^{CYK}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{\includegraphics[width]{Integral_quotient.eps}}}}}}}$}\right)$}\right.}\cong\mathcal{D}

satisfies
g\circ f=id_{\mathcal{D}}
. This proves that
f
is faithful and concludes the proof.

∎
